Abstract
Résumé: Cette thèse présente cinq méthodes de compression de réseaux de neurones, en
se concentrant sur le pruning et les approximations de rang réduit. Premièrement, CORING
(effiCient tensOr decomposition-based filteR prunING) est une méthode de pruning efficace
qui utilise la décomposition en valeurs singulières d’ordre supérieur pour préserver la structure tensorielle des filtres convolutionnels, permettant des approximations de rang faible
efficaces. Deuxièmement, SLIMING (Singular vaLues-drIven autoMated filter prunING) est
un cadre piloté par les valeurs singulières qui formalise le pruning comme un problème
d’optimisation en deux étapes, où la configuration et la sélection des filtres sont guidées
par des propriétés spectrales. Troisièmement, NORTON (enhanced Network cOmpRession
through TensOr decompositions and pruNing) est une approche hybride qui combine les
avantages de la décomposition tensorielle et du pruning structuré. Quatrièmement, CoDeC
(Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation) utilise la décomposition canonique polyadique couplée pour factoriser conjointement des groupes de filtres
similaires, avec une métrique de similarité personnalisée utilisée pour le pré-clustering. Enfin, Flex étend la compression aux fonctions multivariées à valeurs matricielles via un cadre
de découplage formulé comme un problème de factorisation matrice–tenseur couplée, résolu par moindres carrés alternés utilisant des informations d’ordre zéro et un. Ensemble,
ces contributions offrent des stratégies complémentaires et rigoureuses pour réduire la complexité des modèles tout en maintenant des performances élevées sur diverses architectures
de réseaux de neurones.
i
Abstract: This thesis introduces five methods for neural network compression, focusing on pruning and low-rank approximations. First, CORING (effiCient tensOr decompositionbased filteR prunING) is an efficient pruning method that uses higher-order singular value
decomposition to preserve the tensorial structure of convolutional filters, enabling effective low-rank approximations. Second, SLIMING (Singular vaLues-drIven autoMated filter
prunING) is a singular value-driven framework that formulates pruning as a two-stage optimization problem, where both configuration and filter selection are guided by spectral properties. Third, NORTON (enhanced Network cOmpRession through TensOr decompositions
and pruNing) is a hybrid approach that leverages the advantages of both tensor decomposition and structured pruning. Fourth, CoDeC (Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation) employs coupled canonical polyadic decomposition to jointly
factorize groups of similar filters, with a custom similarity metric used for pre-clustering.
Finally, Flex extends compression to matrix-valued multivariate functions through a decoupling framework formulated as a coupled matrix–tensor factorization problem, solved via
alternating least squares with zeroth- and first-order information. Together, these contributions offer complementary and principled strategies to reduce model complexity while
maintaining high performance across diverse neural architectures.
– ii –

– xiv –
Résumé Étendu
Introduction
Au cours de la dernière décennie, l’intelligence artificielle a connu une transformation sans
précédent, portée par l’essor du deep learning [1]. Cette révolution a été rendue possible
grâce à la disponibilité de jeux de données massifs, aux progrès du matériel de calcul (GPU,
TPU, calcul distribué) ainsi qu’au développement d’architectures neuronales de plus en plus
sophistiquées. Ces avancées ont permis d’atteindre des performances de pointe dans de
nombreux domaines, notamment la vision par ordinateur, le traitement automatique du
langage, la reconnaissance vocale et la robotique [2].
Cependant, ces succès se sont accompagnés d’une augmentation considérable de la taille
et de la complexité des modèles, entraînant des coûts élevés en mémoire, en calcul et en
énergie, et posant de réels défis pour leur déploiement, en particulier sur des équipements
embarqués [3], [4]. Ces limitations ont motivé l’émergence de la compression de réseaux
de neurones, un champ de recherche visant à réduire la complexité des modèles tout en
préservant leur performance.
Les principales techniques de compression incluent l’élagage (suppression de poids, filtres ou canaux redondants), la quantification (réduction de la précision numérique), les
décompositions tensorielles (approximation par structures de rang faible), la distillation de
connaissances (apprentissage d’un modèle compact à partir d’un modèle enseignant) ainsi
que la recherche d’architectures neuronales. Parmi elles, l’élagage structuré et les décompositions de tenseurs occupent une place particulière, car elles permettent de réduire la
complexité tout en restant compatibles avec les matériels standards [5], [6].
Malgré ces avancées, plusieurs défis persistent: trouver un compromis entre le ratio
de compression et la précision, adapter les méthodes aux différentes architectures (CNN,
Transformers, MLP-Mixers), prendre en compte des contraintes matérielles et énergétiques,
ainsi que développer des cadres unifiés combinant plusieurs techniques. De plus, l’absence
de standards d’évaluation limite la comparabilité et la reproductibilité des résultats.
Face à ces enjeux, cette thèse propose de nouvelles approches de compression des réseaux
de neurones, fondées sur l’élagage structuré, la décomposition tensorielle et des méthodes
hybrides intégrant les deux paradigmes. L’objectif est de concevoir des techniques efficaces,
générales et mathématiquement fondées, permettant de réduire significativement les coûts
de calcul et de mémoire tout en conservant de la précision.
Les contributions de cette thèse sont les suivantes:
• CORING: Une méthode d’élagage basée sur la factorisation tensorielle qui exploite la
décomposition HOSVD des filtres convolutionnels. En préservant la structure multidimensionnelle inhérente aux filtres, CORING produit des approximations de rang
xv
faible qui maintiennent la précision tout en réduisant significativement la complexité. Un nouveau critère d’élagage basé sur la mesure de bruit est introduit et évalué
sur plusieurs tâches de vision par ordinateur.
• SLIMING: Une approche d’élagage automatique guidée par les valeurs singulières,
qui détermine à la fois les configurations d’élagage et la sélection des filtres sur la
base d’une analyse spectrale. Cette méthode fournit une façon plus rigoureuse et
automatisée d’identifier les redondances pour l’élagage structuré.
• NORTON: Une méthode hybride de compression qui intègre la décomposition tensorielle et l’élagage structuré via le concept de décomposition de filtres. Cette approche permet un contrôle fin du compromis entre le taux de compression et la précision du modèle à travers diverses architectures et ensembles de données.
• CoDeC: Une stratégie de décomposition couplée qui factorise conjointement des groupes
de filtres similaires en utilisant une CPD couplée. Cette méthode propose également
un regroupement personnalisé précédé de la décomposition, améliorant la compacité
et réduisant la redondance.
• Flex: Une méthode générale pour la compression de fonctions matricielles, étendant
les approches traditionnelles de découplage des fonctions vectorielles aux fonctions
matricielles. Formulée comme une factorisation matricielle–tensorielle couplée et résolue par un algorithme des moindres carrés alternés exploitant à la fois l’information
du zéroième et du premier ordre, Flex permet de compresser des architectures au-delà
des CNNs, telles que les Transformers et les MLP-Mixers.
• Revue des décompositions tensorielles pour la compression des réseaux de neurones:
Un état de l’art complet des techniques de décomposition tensorielle appliquées aux
réseaux de neurones profonds, couvrant leurs fondements mathématiques, leurs implémentations pratiques et leurs performances comparatives dans les tâches de compression. Ce survoi consolide les travaux existants, identifie les défis ouverts et esquisse les directions futures de recherche.
Nous résumons ci-dessous un CNN classique qui sera considéré pour la compression.
Considérons un CNN de L couches convolutionnelles. La l-ième couche, pour 1 ≤ l ≤ L,

– xvi –
Élagage Efficace des Filtres Basé sur les Décompositions
Tensorielles
Dans ce travail, nous présentons CORING (effiCient tensOr decomposition-based filteR
prunING), une méthode novatrice de pruning de filtres pour la compression des réseaux
neuronaux. CORING repose sur l’utilisation de décompositions tensorielles, notamment la
HOSVD, permettant de préserver la structure multidimensionnelle des filtres tout en fournissant une approximation de faible rang, réduisant ainsi significativement la complexité.
Cette approche permet également de calculer efficacement la similarité entre deux filtres à
partir de leur approximation de faible rang, sans avoir besoin de redimensionner ceux-ci.
Chaque filtre W i ∈ RI×D×D est décomposé selon HOSVD avec un rang R1 = R2 =
R3 = 1, conduisant à:

où ui ∈ RI , ui , ui ∈ RD sont les vecteurs singuliers dominants issus des matrices dépliées. Cette approximation constitue la meilleure approximation de rang-1 selon la norme
de Frobenius (théorème d’Eckart-Young), tout en étant très efficace en termes de complexité
computationnelle.
Pour mesurer la similarité entre filtres, nous utilisons notamment la distance VBD, inspirée à partir d’une mesure de bruit, définie par:

En considérant la similarité comme l’inverse de cette distance, une matrice de similarité S
est construite pour guider la sélection des filtres à conserver ou à supprimer.
Le processus de pruning repose sur un algorithme itératif qui supprime les filtres les
plus redondants en fonction de la similarité, et sur une stratégie dite de l’élagage de K
fois, qui procède en K étapes successives avec possibilité de entrainage de précision entre
chaque étape, assurant un contrôle précis de la complexité et de la performance du modèle
final.
CORING a été évalué sur diverses tâches de vision par ordinateur, incluant la classification d’images, la détection d’objets, la segmentation d’instances et la détection de points
clés. Les résultats expérimentaux montrent que CORING réduit efficacement le nombre de
MACs et de paramètres tout en maintenant, voire améliorant, la précision de validation par
rapport aux méthodes SOTA. Les résultats qualitatifs confirment également la capacité de
CORING à préserver les caractéristiques essentielles des réseaux neuronaux après l’élagage.

Élagage Automatique de Filtres Guidé par les Valeurs Singulières
Dans cette section, nous présentons SLIMING (Singular vaLues-drIven autoMated filter
prunING), une méthode originale d’élagage automatique de filtres exploitant les valeurs singulières pour formaliser le problème comme une optimisation contrainte sur les tenseurs
de filtres. Cette approche vise à maximiser l’indépendance des filtres conservés tout en
respectant une contrainte de budget global (nombre de filtres, paramètres ou MACs).
Nous mettons en évidence le lien entre la redondance des filtres et la décroissance rapide
de leurs valeurs singulières. En particulier, selon le théorème d’Eckart–Young, la meilleure
approximation de rang r d’une matrice A s’écrit

des filtres de leur sélection fine, tout en autorisant l’usage d’algorithmes heuristiques parallélisables.
Nous présentons l’algorithme 2, conçu pour déterminer la configuration optimale de filtres dans toutes les couches tout en respectant une contrainte sur le nombre total de filtres
retenus, comme décrit dans (7). Le principe central consiste à sélectionner itérativement
les plus grandes valeurs singulières de chaque couche jusqu’à atteindre le nombre de filtres souhaité. Chaque couche commence avec un filtre retenu, et l’ensemble S contient
les prochaines plus grandes valeurs singulières à considérer. À chaque itération, la plus
grande valeur singulière de S est choisie, et le compteur de filtres retenus de la couche correspondante est incrémenté. Ce processus se répète jusqu’à ce que le nombre total de filtres
retenus soit atteint. Appliqué à SVGG, GAM fournit une estimation précise des ratios de
pruning avec une complexité efficace de O(N − L), indépendante de la taille des tenseurs
de poids.
L’algorithme 3, quant à lui, se concentre sur la sélection des filtres à conserver pour
chaque couche, en supposant que la configuration optimale (nombre de filtres par couche)
est déjà connue, conformément à (8). Son objectif est d’identifier, pour chaque couche l,
le sous-ensemble Kl de taille Nl qui maximise l’indépendance des filtres, en minimisant la
redondance tout en préservant la capacité prédictive du réseau. Pour cela, GEM applique
une stratégie heuristique d’élimination itérative: à partir de l’ensemble complet des filtres, il
supprime progressivement les filtres les moins contributifs, en se basant sur l’impact sur la
norme nucléaire globale, jusqu’à obtenir les Nl filtres les plus significatifs. Cette approche
permet de gérer efficacement la nature combinatoire du problème tout en conservant la
performance du réseau.
Compression Améliorée de Réseaux en S’appuyant sur la
Décomposition Tensorielle et l’Élagage
Dans cette section, nous présentons NORTON (enhanced Network cOmpRession through
TensOr decompositions and pruNing), une approche de compression de réseaux combinant
décomposition de tenseurs et élagage structuré. NORTON repose sur la décomposition de
filtres, qui permet une représentation plus fine des poids tout en conservant leurs propriétés multidimensionnelles, et sur un élagage structuré utilisant les matrices factorielles
obtenues.
L’observation clé est que les poids possèdent à la fois des structures à faible rang et des
propriétés de sparsité [7], [8]. Les approches classiques de décomposition ne suppriment
pas tous les canaux redondants, tandis que l’élagage classique peut négliger la structure
faible-rang. En combinant ces deux techniques, NORTON atteint des taux de compression
remarquables, par exemple, pour VGG-16, une compression simultanée de 99% des MACs
et des paramètres avec une perte minime de précision.
La méthode NORTON repose sur un pipeline en deux phases principales: décomposition
et élagage. D’abord, le modèle original est décomposé en CPDBlock via la décomposition
en CPD. Chaque filtre W i d’une couche de convolution est considéré comme un tenseur

d’ordre 3 et décomposé via CPD en trois matrices factorielles:

Le choix du rang R est guidé par la borne de Kruskal R ≤ min{I · D, D2 }. La décomposition réduit le nombre de paramètres de O · I · D2 à O · R · (I + 2D) et la complexité de
calcul de O(I · O · D2 · H · W ) à O(R · O · (I + 2D) · H · W ).
L’élagage de CPDBlock vise à réduire le nombre de paramètres en supprimant les matrices de facteurs obtenues par la décomposition. Chaque sortie O k dépend de trois matrices
Ak , Bk , et Ck , et la suppression d’un filtre nécessite de prendre en compte ces trois matrices simultanément. Comme le CPD est unique à des ambiguïtés d’échelle et de permutation, deux filtres identiques W i = [[Ai , Bi , Ci ]] et W j = [[Aj , Bj , Cj ]] peuvent différer
par Ai = Aj ΠΛA , Bi = Bj ΠΛB , et Ci = Cj ΠΛC avec ΛA ΛB ΛC = I. Pour gérer ces
ambiguïtés et identifier les filtres redondants, nous utilisons la distance PABS ϕ(., .), qui satisfait ϕ(Ai , Aj ) = ϕ(Bi , Bj ) = ϕ(Ci , Cj ) = 0 pour des filtres identiques. L’élagage repose
C

sur la construction d’une matrice de distances D ∈ RO×O , où Dij = αDA

avec α + β + γ = 1, et Dij = ϕ(Ai , Aj ) (de même pour B et C). La procédure itérative
identifie les paires (i, j) minimisant Dij et supprime la décomposition la plus redondante,
garantissant que la représentation conservée reste la plus représentative de l’ensemble.
La

stratégie est détaillée dans l’Algorithme 5 et sa complexité est O O2 · (O − N ) , liée aux
comparaisons par paires et aux itérations de suppression.
En combinant la décomposition fine des filtres et l’élagage structuré, NORTON atteint
un compromis très bon entre compression et précision. La factorisation des filtres offre un
contrôle précis sur la granularité de la compression et simplifie la sélection des rangs, tandis
que l’élagage basé sur les matrices factorielles élimine les redondances. Des expérimentations sur des architectures représentatives et des tâches différentes de vision démontrent
que NORTON surpasse les méthodes existantes en termes de réduction de complexité et de
préservation de la précision.
– xx –
Décomposition Tensorielle Couplée pour la Représentation Compacte des Réseaux
Cette section présente l’approche CoDeC (Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation), basée sur une observation que des redondances existent entre les filtres d’une couche convolutionnelle. En effet, des filtres similaires peuvent produire des sorties partiellement redondantes. Pour exploiter cette propriété, nous proposons une décomposition conjointe des filtres via des décompositions tensorielles couplées, spécifiquement la
CPD couplée, permettant le partage d’une matrice de facteurs commune entre filtres similaires. Cette factorisation conjointe réduit non seulement le nombre de paramètres, mais
diminue également la complexité computationnelle en éliminant les calculs redondants.
Les approches de compression de réseaux reposant sur des approximations de rang
faible exploitent la faible dimensionnalité des poids en les factorisant. Les méthodes existantes peuvent être classées en deux catégories: les décompositions matricielles et les
décompositions tensorielles. Les méthodes matricielles [9], [10], [11], [12], [13], [14], [15],
[16] aplatissent le tenseur de poids de 4ème ordre en matrice pour appliquer la SVD, ce qui
peut entraîner une perte de la structure multidimensionnelle. Les approches tensorielles
comme CPD [17], [18], TD [3], [19], [20], [21] ou TTD [22] conservent cette structure, capturant des informations complexes à haut ordre présentes dans les paramètres. Les décompositions couplées permettent de factoriser simultanément plusieurs matrices ou tenseurs
liés, exploitant des structures communes. Pour la compression de CNN, des travaux récents
ont appliqué la TTD ou la SVD couplée [23], [24], mais la réduction de la complexité reste
limitée si les entrées diffèrent entre couches.
Plutôt que de décomposer chaque filtre indépendamment, nous proposons une factorisation conjointe le long d’un mode spécifique. Chaque filtre conserve ses matrices de facteurs propres pour certains modes, mais partage une matrice de facteurs commune pour le
mode couplé. Cela permet de calculer une fois la multiplication entre la matrice commune
et le tenseur d’entrée, réduisant considérablement la complexité.
Un élément critique est le choix des filtres à coupler. Coupler tous les filtres peut être
trop restrictif et dégrader la précision. Nous introduisons donc une étape de regroupement
(clustering) préalable: les filtres sont regroupés selon leur similarité dans l’espace des sousmodules communs. Nous utilisons K-means avec une métrique de distance personnalisée
basée sur les projecteurs orthogonaux des vecteurs singuliers des filtres, ce qui garantit une
mesure robuste des sous-espaces partagés.
CoDeC procède en deux étapes : (i) le regroupement
(clustering) des O filtres en K clusPK
ters, où chaque groupe k contient ok filtres ( k=1 ok = O), et (ii) la décomposition couplée
(CPD couplée) des filtres de chaque groupe. Le regroupement est réalisé via K-means en
utilisant une distance projetée basée sur les matrices de vecteurs singuliers gauches Ui et
Uj des déroulement mode-1 des filtres, avec la métrique d(Ui , Uj ) = Ui U†i − Uj U†j ,
F
invariante aux transformations dans le sous-espace, permettant de comparer efficacement
des filtres similaires malgré les ambiguïtés de permutation et de mise à l’échelle.
Pour un cluster contenant o filtres, les filtres sont représentés par
W i = [[A, Bi , Ci ]],

o1 , · · · , O 1 , . . . , O oK
(20)
(21)
(22)
(23)
avec X ∈ RHin ×Win ×(R·K) , Y ∈ RHout ×Win ×(R·O) , Z ∈ RHout ×Wout ×(R·O) , et O ∈ RHout ×Wout ×O .
Cette structure, appelée CoDeCBlock, peut être directement implémentée dans les frameworks de deep learning classiques. L’approche couplée réduit la complexité et la mémoire en partageant la matrice de facteur commune A, et permet une accélération pratique
d’environ 8× par rapport à la convolution non couplée dans un exemple avec O = 1024,
K = 128.
Le rang R et le nombre de clusters K déterminent le taux de compression. Le nombre de
paramètres original d’une couche est O·I·D2 , et pour le CoDeCBlock: R·(I · K + O · 2D) <
O · I · D2 . La sélection du rang en décomposition tensorielle est un problème NP-difficile,
et des heuristiques comme CORCONDIA peuvent être utilisées pour guider ce choix. Dans
notre contexte, le rang est avant tout un paramètre de conception permettant de contrôler le
compromis entre compression et précision, plutôt qu’une valeur à interprétation physique.
Découplage des Fonctions Matricielles à l’Aide des Informations du Premier et du Zéro-Ordre
L’approximation des fonctions non linéaires est essentielle dans de nombreux domaines
scientifiques et d’ingénierie, notamment pour l’apprentissage profond et l’identification de
– xxii –
systèmes non linéaires. Le découplage des fonctions multivariées, qui exprime une fonction
multivariée comme une combinaison linéaire de fonctions univariées appliquées à des projections linéaires des entrées, permet de concevoir des fonctions d’activation flexibles et
des modèles économes en paramètres. Cependant, les approches existantes se limitent aux
fonctions vectorielles, ce qui empêche leur application à des architectures modernes telles
que les Transformers ou les architectures basées sur MLP, qui manipulent des données matricielles.
Pour combler cette lacune, dans ce travail, nous formalisons le problème du découplage
de fonctions à valeurs matricielles comme un problème de CMTF. Nous proposons ensuite
de le résoudre via l’algorithme ALS en utilisant à la fois l’information du premier et du
zéro ordre de la fonction. L’application du découplage de fonctions à valeurs matricielles
peut être variée, par exemple en traitement du signal, en identification de systèmes ou en
ingénierie de contrôle, mais nous nous concentrons particulièrement sur son application à
la compression de Deep Neural Networks (DNNs). Plus précisément, nous l’utilisons pour
compresser des architectures à valeurs matricielles telles que les DNNs basés sur Transformer [2] et les DNNs basés sur MLP [25], [26]. À travers des expériences étendues sur
quatre architectures et sept jeux de données, l’efficacité de notre approche est démontrée
en termes de précision, réduction du nombre de paramètres et réduction des MACs, montrant sa supériorité par rapport aux méthodes concurrentes. Par exemple, sur ImageNet
avec DeiT, notre méthode obtient une réduction de paramètres supérieure de 7% comparée
à SPViT-T [27], tout en améliorant la précision de 0,28%.
À notre connaissance, notre approche est la première à généraliser le découplage de
fonctions au cadre à valeurs matricielles et à démontrer son utilité pratique pour la compression de DNNs matricielles à grande échelle. Elle présente plusieurs différences clés par
rapport aux travaux existants. Premièrement, comparée aux cadres de découplage précédents [28], [29], [30], notre méthode prend en charge des entrées et sorties à valeurs matricielles. Deuxièmement, contrairement aux méthodes standard d’approximation de rang
faible [31], [32], [33] qui décomposent les poids des couches, notre approche approxime la
fonction qui relie l’entrée à la sortie, sans accéder directement aux poids. Troisièmement,
contrairement à l’élagage ou à la quantification, notre méthode agit en approximant la fonction elle-même via une décomposition. Cela implique une stratégie différente et peut être
combinée avec ces méthodes pour obtenir une compression encore meilleure.
En résumé, les contributions de ce travail sont doubles. Tout d’abord, nous formalisons le découplage de fonctions à valeurs matricielles comme un problème de CMTF et
proposons une solution basée sur ALS exploitant à la fois l’information du zéro et du premier ordre. Ensuite, nous montrons son application à la compression de DNNs, en évaluant
notre méthode sur quatre architectures et sept jeux de données et en la comparant avec les
travaux connexes.

Conclusions et Perspectives
Cette thèse a traité les défis importants de la compression des réseaux neuronaux profonds
dans un contexte d’augmentation de la taille des modèles, des besoins en calcul et des contraintes de déploiement. Nous avons proposé une série d’approches novatrices pour la
compression structurée, fondées sur les principes mathématiques des approximations de
rang faible et de l’élagage d’un réseau de neurones.
Nos contributions s’étendent sur plusieurs directions complémentaires. Tout d’abord,
nous avons développé CORING, une méthode d’élagage basée sur la factorisation tensorielle
exploitant la structure HOSVD des filtres convolutionnels. En préservant la structure multidimensionnelle intrinsèque à ces filtres, CORING permet une compression efficace avec
une dégradation minime des performances. Ensuite, nous avons proposé SLIMING, une
méthode d’élagage automatique des filtres basée sur l’information spectrale. Cette approche utilise l’analyse des valeurs singulières pour guider la configuration d’élagage et la
sélection des filtres, offrant ainsi un processus de compression plus efficace et automatisé.
Troisièmement, nous avons introduit NORTON, une méthode hybride combinant décomposition tensorielle et l’élagage structuré via la notion de décomposition de filtres. Cette
méthode permet un compromis fin entre le taux de compression et la précision du modèle sur différentes architectures et jeux de données. Quatrièmement, nous avons présenté
CoDeC, une stratégie de décomposition couplée qui factorise conjointement des groupes de
filtres similaires en utilisant un CPD couplé. Un algorithme de regroupement sur mesure
est utilisé pour regrouper les filtres avant la décomposition, améliorant la compacité de
représentation et réduisant la redondance. Enfin, nous avons proposé Flex, une méthode générale pour l’approximation de fonctions matricielles, permettant la compression
d’architectures au-delà des CNNs, telles que Transformers et MLP-Mixers. Cette méthode formalise le problème de compression comme une factorisation matricielle-tensorielle
couplée et le résout via un algorithme des moindres carrés alternés intégrant à la fois les
informations d’ordre zéro et premier.
– xxvi –
Les protocoles d’évaluation, systématiquement appliqués, garantissent une comparaison équitable entre les méthodes et confirment la généralité et l’efficacité des approches
proposées. Ces travaux contribuent à rendre les modèles d’apprentissage profond plus efficaces, évolutifs et adaptés aux applications réelles, tout en maintenant leur précision et
leur généralisation.
Plusieurs pistes prometteuses se dégagent pour la recherche future:
• Optimisation automatique des hyperparamètres de compression: l’utilisation
de méthodes comme le NAS ou l’apprentissage par renforcement pourrait automatiser la sélection optimale des paramètres (taux d’élagage, rangs de décomposition,
nombre de clusters ou de fonctions de base).
• Extension à des architectures variées: la généralité de l’approche Flex permet son
application à des modèles comme Transformers et MLP-Mixers, notamment pour le
traitement du langage, l’analyse de séries temporelles ou l’apprentissage multimodal.
• Cadres hybrides de compression: combiner l’élagage, la factorisation tensorielle,
la distillation, la quantification et la NAS dans un même cadre pourrait augmenter les
taux de compression tout en préservant la précision.
• Compression consciente du matériel: intégrer explicitement les contraintes matérielles
(mémoire, parallélisme, consommation énergétique) pour optimiser un déploiement
en pratique.
• Compression pendant l’entraînement, continue et fédérée: développer des stratégies adaptatives et légères de compression durant l’entraînement, particulièrement
pour l’apprentissage continu et/ou fédéré, afin de réduire la surcharge communicationnelle et gérer efficacement la capacité des modèles.
En résumé, cette thèse propose des techniques de compression efficaces et mathématiquement fondées, mais le paysage de la compression de modèles continue d’évoluer. La
diversité croissante des architectures, tâches et environnements de déploiement rend nécessaire le développement de solutions flexibles, adaptatives et théoriquement justifiées.
– xxvii –
Chapter 1
Introduction
The past decade has witnessed an unprecedented transformation in artificial intelligence,
largely driven by the rapid rise of deep learning [1]. Enabled by the availability of largescale datasets, advances in computational hardware, and increasingly sophisticated neural
network architectures, deep learning has achieved state-of-the-art performance across computer vision, natural language processing, speech recognition, robotics, and many other domains [2]. This progress has been accompanied by a steady increase in model size and complexity, leading to remarkable gains in accuracy and generalization, but also raising new
challenges related to efficiency, scalability, and deployment [6], [34], [35]. In this chapter, we first examine the historical and technological factors that fueled the rise of deep
learning, then discuss the pressing need for model compression, the main challenges and
research gaps in the field, the specific aims of this thesis, and finally outline the manuscript
structure.
1.1
The Rise of Deep Learning
In recent years, deep learning has become a central paradigm in artificial intelligence, driving breakthroughs across diverse domains such as computer vision, natural language processing, robotics, and speech recognition [1]. Its success stems from a convergence of multiple key factors: the availability of large-scale datasets (both labeled and unlabeled), the rapid
growth in computational resources—particularly Graphics Processing Units (GPUs), Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), and distributed training frameworks—and the development
of efficient software libraries such as PyTorch and TensorFlow [36], [37]. Equally important are advances in neural network architectures (e.g., CNNs, Recurrent Neural Networks
(RNNs), Transformers), optimization algorithms (e.g., Adam, RMSProp), and regularization
techniques (e.g., Batch Normalization, Dropout) [2], [38], [39]. The rise of transfer learning,
large-scale pretraining, and open access to prebuilt models and datasets has further accelerated progress, making deep learning a flexible and powerful framework across a wide
range of applications [1], [2], [38], [40].
Historically, the concept of neural networks dates back to the 1950s, with early models
1
Chapter 1
such as the perceptron, but these architectures were shallow and limited in their capacity to
model complex, high-dimensional data. The modern resurgence of deep learning began in
the early 2010s, catalyzed by the success of AlexNet, which achieved a decisive victory in the
ImageNet large-scale visual recognition challenge by leveraging deep convolutional layers
and GPU acceleration [1]. This marked a paradigm shift, demonstrating the effectiveness
of hierarchical feature learning and establishing the feasibility of training deep networks
on large-scale datasets [36], [41].
Following this breakthrough, a series of increasingly sophisticated architectures emerged,
including VGGNet, GoogLeNet (Inception), ResNet, DenseNet, MobileNet, and many others
[38], [42], [43], [44], [45]. These models pushed the boundaries of depth, often comprising
tens to hundreds of layers and millions of parameters, while introducing key innovations
such as residual connections, dense connectivity, attention module, and depthwise separable convolution [44], [45]. Although these deeper models achieved state-of-the-art performance across numerous benchmarks, they also introduced significant challenges in terms
of computational cost, memory usage, and energy efficiency [46].
A major paradigm shift occurred in 2017 with the introduction of the Transformer architecture, which replaced recurrence and convolutions with self-attention mechanisms [2].
Initially designed for machine translation, Transformers quickly became the foundation
for a wide array of natural language processing models, including BERT, and GPT series,
trained on massive corpora like Wikipedia, BooksCorpus, and Common Crawl [47], [48].
More recently, Transformer variants have been adapted for computer vision tasks (e.g.,
Vision Transformer (ViT)) and multimodal models (e.g., CLIP) [49]. These models demonstrate remarkable scalability and generalization, enabling zero-shot, few-shot, and transfer
learning capabilities [47], [50], [51].
Deep learning systems are typically trained on curated datasets that range from foundational benchmarks like MNIST to large-scale datasets such as CIFAR-10/100, ImageNet,
COCO, SQuAD, and GLUE [36], [41], [52], [53]. Training typically relies on gradient-based
optimization (e.g., SGD, Adam) and techniques like data augmentation, batch normalization, dropout, and learning rate scheduling to improve generalization and training stability
[39], [54]. These advances have collectively enabled deep learning to outperform traditional
machine learning in a wide range of domains [2], [38], [55].
1.2
The Need for Model Compression
Despite their impressive performance, modern deep learning models are often prohibitively
large, with hundreds of millions or even billions of parameters. This trend toward overparameterization, while beneficial for generalization and transfer learning, introduces several
practical drawbacks. These include high memory and storage requirements, increased inference latency, substantial energy consumption, and deployment challenges, particularly
on resource-constrained devices such as mobile phones, embedded systems, and IoT platforms [3], [4].
Consequently, there has been growing interest in model compression, a field that seeks
–2–
Introduction
to reduce the computational and storage footprint of neural networks while preserving (or
minimally degrading) their performance. Moreover, compression is essential for enabling
on-device artificial intelligence and for complying with sustainability and green artificial
intelligence goals by reducing the energy footprint of deep learning applications.
Model compression encompasses a diverse set of techniques, including:
• Pruning, which removes unnecessary weights, filters, or channels from the model
[6];
• Quantization, which reduces the numerical precision of weights and activations to
lower bit-width representations [56];
• Tensor decompositions, which approximate high-dimensional tensors (e.g., convolutional kernels) by lower-rank structures [5];
• Knowledge distillation, where a smaller model learns to replicate the outputs of a
larger teacher model [57].
• Neural Architecture Search (NAS), which automatically explores efficient model architectures tailored for target hardware and tasks [58].
Among these, structured pruning and low-rank tensor decomposition are of particular
interest due to their compatibility with standard hardware and potential to preserve computational efficiency without requiring specialized sparse computation kernels. Structured
pruning techniques remove groups of parameters (e.g., entire filters or channels), producing smaller, dense networks. Tensor decomposition methods, such as CPD, Tucker, and
SVD, factorize weight tensors into compact representations, leading to fewer parameters
and reduced floating-point operations [5], [6].
These approaches are increasingly relevant as modern deep networks continue to grow
in size and complexity, such as in Transformer-based models in NLP (e.g., BERT, GPT),
and hybrid architectures in vision (e.g., Vision Transformers, MLP-Mixers). Compression
not only facilitates deployment in real-world systems but also offers insights into network
interpretability, efficiency, and generalization [5], [6].
1.3
Challenges and Research Gaps
Nevertheless, compressing deep neural networks is not a trivial task. It involves intricate
trade-offs between model size, accuracy, training time, and hardware constraints. A major
challenge lies in identifying and removing redundant components of the model without
compromising its ability to generalize. Many compression techniques, such as pruning or
low-rank factorizations, introduce a new set of hyperparameters (e.g., pruning ratio, decomposition rank) whose tuning is non-trivial and often requires extensive experimentation or
domain expertise [59].
–3–
Chapter 1
Another complication arises from the fact that the effectiveness of compression strategies depends heavily on the specific architecture and task. For example, techniques that
perform well on convolutional neural networks may not transfer effectively to models based
on the Transformer architecture or to recurrent neural networks. In addition, certain methods tend to experience substantial performance degradation when applied to models that
are already highly optimized or compact by design, such as SqueezeNet, MobileNet, or EfficientNet [45], [60], [61].
The deployment environment adds another layer of complexity. Compressed models
must often meet strict latency, energy, and memory requirements imposed by edge devices,
mobile processors, or specialized accelerators. However, certain compression strategies,
such as unstructured pruning, may yield models that are sparse in theory but not practically efficient on existing hardware due to poor support for sparse matrix operations. This
necessitates hardware-aware compression approaches that explicitly account for the target
platform’s execution model and memory architecture [62].
From a theoretical perspective, the compression process can destabilize the optimization
landscape. Some techniques—especially when applied post hoc on pretrained models—may
degrade convergence properties or introduce sharp minima, which adversely affect generalization. Retraining or fine-tuning is often required after compression to recover performance, leading to increased computational overhead [63].
Furthermore, combining multiple compression techniques (e.g., pruning and tensor decompositions) poses additional integration challenges. The interactions between different
methods are not always well understood, and naively stacking them may result in suboptimal performance or training instability. There is a growing interest in developing unified
frameworks or joint optimization strategies that can harmonize these methods under a
common objective [8].
Finally, the lack of robust evaluation standards remains a major barrier. Compression
results are often reported under inconsistent metrics—e.g., parameter count, MACs, latency,
or energy usage—on diverse hardware backends, making it difficult to compare methods
fairly. Reproducibility is further hindered by the absence of standardized benchmarks and
toolchains for measuring actual speedups and deployment performance [64], [65].
Overall, these challenges highlight the need for more principled, automated, and hardwareaware compression techniques that can generalize across architectures and tasks, while
ensuring deployment viability and performance consistency.
1.4
Aims and Contributions of the Thesis
In response to the challenges identified in model compression, this thesis explores novel
approaches to model compression, with a focus on structured pruning, tensor decomposition, and hybrid techniques that integrate both paradigms. Our goal is to develop methods
that are not only effective in reducing computational and memory costs, but also general,
scalable, and grounded in solid mathematical foundations.
–4–
Introduction
To achieve these goals, the thesis proposes a series of complementary approaches, including filter pruning guided by tensor factorization, singular value-driven pruning strategies, a hybrid framework that combines decomposition with pruning, and compact network
representation using coupled tensor decomposition. Additionally, new formulations extend
traditional decomposition methods to matrix-valued functions, enabling more flexible and
powerful compression of modern architectures such as Transformers and MLP-Mixers.
The contributions of this thesis are as follows:
• CORING: A tensor factorization-based pruning method that leverages the HOSVD
structure of convolutional filters. By preserving the filters’ inherent multi-linear
structure, CORING produces low-rank approximations that maintain accuracy while
significantly reducing complexity. A novel similarity-based pruning criterion is introduced, evaluated across multiple computer vision tasks.
• SLIMING: A singular value-driven pruning approach that determines both pruning
configurations and filter selection based on spectral analysis. This method provides
a principled and more automated way to identify redundancy and guide structured
pruning.
• NORTON: A unified compression framework that integrates tensor decomposition
and structured pruning via the concept of filter decomposition. This approach enables
fine-grained control over the trade-off between compression rate and model accuracy
across diverse architectures and datasets.
• CoDeC: A coupled decomposition strategy that jointly factorizes groups of similar
filters using coupled CPD. A custom clustering method precedes decomposition, enhancing compactness and reducing redundancy.
• Flex: A general framework for compressing matrix-valued functions, extending traditional decoupling approaches from the vector-valued to the matrix-valued setting.
Formulated as a coupled matrix–tensor factorization and solved via an alternating
least squares algorithm that leverages both zeroth- and first-order information, Flex
enables the compression of architectures beyond CNNs, such as Transformers and
MLP-Mixers.
• Survey of tensor decomposition for neural network compression: A comprehensive
review of tensor decomposition techniques applied to deep neural networks, covering
their mathematical foundations, practical implementations, and comparative performance in compression tasks. This survey consolidates existing work, identifies open
challenges, and outlines future research directions.
The research carried out during this PhD has resulted in the following publications:

–6–
Introduction
1.5
Manuscript Structure
This manuscript is organized as follows:
• Chapter 1 provides an overview of the motivations behind neural network compression, the associated challenges and existing gaps, and the core objectives of the thesis.
It also outlines the key contributions and the structure of the manuscript.
• Chapter 2 presents essential algebraic definitions, introduces the neural network architectures and datasets used in our experiments, and outlines the evaluation protocols. It surveys recent advances in model compression, with a focus on low-rank
approximations and pruning, and positions the contributions of this thesis within the
current state of the art. Related publication: [J3].
• Chapter 3 introduces the CORING method, which employs HOSVD for filter pruning,
yielding low-rank approximations that preserve the filters’ multidimensional structure. It details the proposed similarity-based pruning criteria and the empirical results
across various computer vision tasks. Related publications: [J2], [C2].
• Chapter 4 presents SLIMING, a method that formulates filter pruning as a two-step
process based on singular value analysis. It explains how pruning configurations and
filter selection are determined automatically using spectral properties and validates
the approach through extensive experiments. Related publication: [J4].
• Chapter 5 describes NORTON, a hybrid framework that integrates tensor decomposition and structured pruning through the notion of filter decomposition. It highlights how the method balances compression and accuracy across diverse models and
datasets. Related publications: [J1], [C1].
• Chapter 6 introduces CoDeC, a coupled tensor decomposition strategy that identifies
and factorizes similar filters jointly using coupled CPD. It also includes a custom
clustering method to enhance decomposition quality and reduce redundancy. Related
publication: [J5].
• Chapter 7 presents Flex, a new paradigm in neural network compression based on
function decoupling. It extends traditional decoupling frameworks—originally designed for vector-valued functions—to the matrix-valued setting. The proposed method
is formulated as a coupled matrix-tensor factorization problem and solved via an alternating least squares algorithm that leverages both zeroth- and first-order information. Experimental evaluations on Transformer and MLP-based architectures across
several benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach in achieving both
high compression and competitive accuracy. Related publication: [J6].
• Chapter 8 summarizes the main contributions of the thesis and outlines some potential directions for future research in neural network compression.
–7–
Chapter 2
Background and Related Works
In this chapter, we provide the necessary theoretical and algorithmic background for the
work presented in subsequent chapters. We begin by introducing key concepts in algebraic
low-rank approximations, which serve as the mathematical basis for several compression
methods explored in this thesis. We then present the neural network architectures studied
in our experiments, including convolutional neural networks, transformer-based models,
and MLP-like architectures. Next, we provide an overview of the datasets and the evaluation protocols used in our experiments. Finally, we review related works on neural network
compression, with a focus on pruning and low-rank decomposition—two techniques central to the methods developed in this thesis—while also discussing other relevant strategies
such as neural architecture search, knowledge distillation, and quantization. This comprehensive overview situates our research within the broader landscape of model compression
and highlights the motivations and design choices behind our proposed methods.
2.1
Background
In this section, we present the foundational concepts for this thesis, covering basic definitions of algebraic low-rank approximations, the architectures of deep neural networks, the
benchmark datasets, and the evaluation protocols employed in our experiments.

Deep Neural Networks
In this subsection, we present the architecture of the DNNs that will be used later in this
thesis. We begin with CNNs, employed in Chapters 3,4,5, and 6, followed by Transformer
and MLP-based architectures, used in Chapter 7.

Transformer and MLP-based architectures
In this subsubsection, we describe the architectures of three matrix-valued neural networks:
Vision Transformer (ViT) [49], Mixer [25], and ResMLP [26]. A common feature among
them is the use of an embedding layer that converts the raw input image into a matrix
U ∈ RL×M , where M denotes the number of image patches and L represents the embedding dimension per patch. This embedded input is then passed through a sequence of
blocks—such as Transformer blocks, Mixer blocks, or ResMLP blocks—depending on the
– 13 –
Chapter 2
architecture. In Chapter 7, our focus is on approximating these blocks, as they embody
the matrix-valued functions of interest. In the following, we provide an overview of these
blocks to show that each block operates as a matrix-valued function, F : RL×M → RL×M .
A ViT block [49] consists of multi-head self-attention and a FFN. For each of the H

Outputs from all heads are concatenated to form A = A(1) , . . . , A(H) , which is then

FFN, consisting of two fully connected layers with weights W1 ∈ R4L×L and W2 ∈ RL×4L ,
processes O to yield the final output Y = W2 ReLU(W1 O) + O.
Mixer [25] is an architecture based purely on MLP, demonstrating that MLP-only models can serve as effective alternatives to convolutional and transformer-based networks.
It comprises per-patch linear embeddings, stacked Mixer layers, and a classification head.
Each Mixer layer includes a token-mixing and a channel-mixing MLP, both consisting of
two fully connected layers with GELU activations. For the input U ∈ RL×M , a Mixer layer
returns the output Y ∈ RL×M as follows
Xl,: = Ul,: + W2 GELU (W1 LayerNorm (Ul,: )) , for l = 1 . . . L,
Y:,m = X:,m + W4 GELU (W3 LayerNorm (X:,m )) , for m = 1 . . . M,

where W1 ∈ R4M ×M , W2 ∈ RM ×4M , W3 ∈ R4L×L , and W4 ∈ RL×4L .
ResMLP [26] is an MLP-based architecture for image classification that eliminates selfattention, positional embeddings, and the additional “class” token used in ViT. It comprises
a linear projection for input embedding, a stack of ResMLP layers, and a classification head.
Each ResMLP layer includes a cross-patch linear sublayer and a cross-channel two-layer
MLP, both equipped with residual connections and affine element-wise transformations.
For a vector u ∈ RL , the affine transformation is defined as Aff a,b (u) = diag(a)u + b,
where a ∈ RL and b ∈ RL are learnable weight vectors. For the input U ∈ RL×M , Aff(U)
denotes the affine operations applied independently to each column of U. A ResMLP layer
performs the following transformations

MNIST (Modified National Institute of Standards and Technology) [52] is a handwritten
digit recognition dataset of 28 × 28 grayscale images spanning 10 classes, and it remains a
fundamental benchmark for testing basic architectures. QMNIST [69] is an extended version of MNIST that provides additional samples and annotations, making it suitable for
more robust evaluation. FashionMNIST [70] replaces digits with grayscale clothing items,
offering a more visually challenging alternative to MNIST while preserving the same format. USPS (United States Postal Service) [71] is another handwritten digit dataset with
smaller 16 × 16 grayscale images, frequently used in domain adaptation studies. SVHN
(Street View House Numbers) consists of digit images cropped from natural scene photographs, representing a more realistic and noisy digit recognition task compared to MNIST.
The CIFAR (Canadian Institute for Advanced Research) datasets [53], CIFAR-10 and
CIFAR-100, contain 32 × 32 natural images across 10 and 100 classes, respectively, and
serve as canonical benchmarks for convolutional architectures and compression studies.
ImageNet [36], a large-scale dataset with over 1.3 million images and 1,000 object categories,
is the standard benchmark for evaluating scalability and generalization in deep learning.
Finally, COCO (Common Objects in Context) [41] contains complex images with multiple
objects per scene, annotated for tasks including detection (80 object categories), instance
segmentation, and human keypoint detection (17 keypoints for the person class).
Small-scale datasets such as MNIST, FashionMNIST, USPS, and SVHN are widely used
for benchmarking lightweight models and studying compression methods due to their simplicity and fast training cycles, which make them ideal for rapid experimentation and ablation studies. CIFAR and QMNIST provide medium-scale challenges with more variability
in image content, serving as standard testbeds for convolutional architectures and prun– 15 –
Chapter 2
ing strategies. ImageNet, with over a million high-resolution images across 1,000 classes,
represents a large-scale benchmark that is indispensable for validating the scalability and
generalization of compression techniques. Finally, COCO extends the evaluation beyond
classification to structured prediction tasks, allowing us to showcase the deployment of
compressed models in real-world scenarios where spatial precision and complex scene understanding are critical. A summary of these datasets is provided in Table 2.1.
2.1.4
Evaluation Protocols
In this subsection, we present the evaluation protocols that are used in this thesis. The
performance of the compressed model is assessed via three criteria: accuracy, required
MACs, and the number of parameters (Parameters). The MACs and parameter counts reflect
the cost of computation and storage consumption. The compression ratio (CR) is quantified
as the percentage reduction in MACs or Parameters compared to the original model as
follows:


compressed
× 100%.
(2.22)
CR = 1 −
original
Concerning the performance, top-1 accuracy on the test dataset is used on classification
tasks while the mean average precision (AP) and recall (AR) are used on the object detection,
instance segmentation, and human keypoint detection tasks [73], [74]. The accuracy drop
is computed as the difference in top-1 accuracy between the compressed model and the
original uncompressed model:
∆accuracy = accuracyoriginal − accuracycompressed .
(2.23)
In all experiments, a good compression method is expected to achieve a high CR with a
minimal accuracy drop, indicating efficient compression with limited impact on task performance. These evaluation protocols serve as a common foundation for experimental comparisons and are consistently applied across all subsequent chapters of this thesis.
2.2
Related Works on Neural Network Compression
In this section, we review the fundamental concepts behind popular model compression
techniques, including pruning, low-rank approximations, NAS, knowledge distillation, and
quantization. We place particular emphasis on pruning and low-rank approximations, as
they form the main focus of this thesis, while the other methods are discussed more briefly
to provide contextual background.
2.2.1
Pruning
Pruning is a foundational technique for neural network compression that aims to eliminate redundant or less important components from a model to reduce its size and com– 16 –
Background and Related Works
putational cost, while preserving performance. The intuition behind pruning is that overparameterized neural networks often contain many weights or neurons that contribute little
to the final prediction and can thus be removed without significantly impacting accuracy
[4].
Pruning can be analyzed across five critical dimensions: what to prune, when to prune,
how to prune, how often to prune, and how to configure pruning.
What to prune. Pruning methods can be broadly categorized into three types: unstructured, semi-structured, and structured pruning. Unstructured pruning removes individual
weights across the network, leading to highly sparse weight matrices. While this can significantly reduce the number of parameters, it often requires specialized hardware or software
to achieve real-world speedups due to the irregular computation patterns. Semi-structured
pruning offers a compromise by removing groups of weights following specific patterns
(e.g., N:M sparsity), enabling partial hardware acceleration while retaining some of the
flexibility of unstructured pruning [75]. Structured pruning, on the other hand, eliminates
entire architectural components such as filters, channels, attention heads, or neurons. This
produces dense, smaller sub-networks that are more amenable to acceleration on conventional hardware and easier to deploy in practical scenarios [4], [76], [77], [78]. In this thesis,
we focus on structured pruning, specifically filter pruning, as it strikes a practical balance
between model compression and hardware efficiency. By removing entire filters, we obtain compact convolutional architectures that are directly compatible with standard deep
learning libraries and inference engines without requiring sparse matrix support or custom
kernels.
How to prune. Undoubtedly, the pruning criterion is the foundation of network pruning
that discriminates methods in the literature. The decision of which components to remove
critically influences the final performance of the pruned model. A wide range of criteria
have been proposed, which can be grouped into several broad families:
• Magnitude-based criteria: One of the most widely used heuristics is to prune weights
or filters with the smallest magnitude, under the assumption that they contribute less
to the network output. In structured pruning, this typically translates to removing
filters with low ℓ1 or ℓ2 norm [4], [65], [77], [79], [80]. Other metrics like the rank
[81], entropy [82], [83], [84], torque [85], and energy [86] are also used to gauge
filter saliency. These methods are simple, computationally efficient, and often yield
competitive performance, especially in early-stage pruning research [4].
• Similarity-based criteria: These methods prune redundant components by measuring similarity between filters or activation patterns. Highly correlated or similar
units are considered less informative and are removed to promote diversity among
the remaining ones [82], [87], [88], [89], [90], [91], [92], [93], [94], [95], [96], [97],
[98]. This strategy is particularly effective in overparameterized convolutional layers. Techniques such as similarity-based pruning [82], [87], [89], [92], [94], [97], [98],
[99] assess filter importance through pairwise distance or correlation metrics. Some
works introduce measures like channel independence [82], [88], [91], [93], [96], filter
divergence [97], or attention map similarity [90], [100]. Clustering-based pruning
methods such as SFP [87], ICP [99], and EACP [101] also fall under this category,
– 17 –
Chapter 2
where filters are grouped by similarity and only representative ones are retained,
further encouraging diversity and efficiency.
• Sensitivity-based and loss-aware criteria: Sensitivity-based approaches evaluate parameter importance based on the sensitivity of the loss function to parameter removal [102], [103]. ASTER [102] improves pruning stability by dynamically adjusting
the threshold via sensitivity analysis on batch normalization layers, enabling a better trade-off between pruning ratio and accuracy. SP [103] extends a saliency-based
pruning metric to a structured setting, combining sensitivity scores with fine-tuning
to improve pruning efficiency under training dynamics. Loss-aware approaches estimate the direct impact of pruning on the validation loss or performance metrics [76],
[104], [105], [106]. Molchanov et al. [104] proposed a first-order Taylor expansion to
estimate the impact of pruning on the loss, enabling effective pruning during transfer
learning with minimal accuracy degradation. GFBS [105] introduces a gradient flowbased saliency criterion that incorporates the effects of normalization and activation
layers via a Taylor expansion, enabling more effective channel pruning by leveraging
first-order approximations of gradient flow through batch normalization parameters.
• Optimization-based criteria: In this line of work, pruning is cast as an optimization
problem [65], [93], [107], [108], [109]. For instance, DAIS [107] formulates channel
pruning as a bi-level optimization problem using differentiable and annealed channel indicators, effectively navigating the large pruning search space. ResPrune [65]
introduces a two-phase pruning strategy that alternates between pruning based on
norm and redundancy criteria and restoring filters stochastically to improve convergence. UDSP [108] unifies static and dynamic channel pruning through a bi-level optimization framework, reducing both inference cost and storage. CATRO [93] models
pruning as a class-aware submodular optimization problem, jointly considering the
global importance of channels for task-specific pruning.
• Architecture search-based criteria: This line of work formulates pruning as a search
problem over pruning strategies, where pruning ratios and/or criteria are automatically discovered using neural architecture search, reinforcement learning, or evolutionary algorithms. These methods often aim to balance accuracy and resource constraints without relying on fixed heuristics. Notable examples include EvoFC [110],
DAIS [107], Lu et al. [59], Lee et al. [111], ABCPruner [112], RLAL [113], ATO [63],
GCPNet [114], SAESA [115] and MCMC [116].
• Other criteria: Graph-based approaches, such as HSC [117], RGP [118], and redundancy graph decomposition [92], model complex high-order relationships among filters using spectral clustering, graph topology, or hypergraph learning to better capture redundancy. Attention-based methods like ACSC [100] and GlobalPru [119] use
attention mechanisms to adaptively guide structured pruning. Interpretable pruning
methods, such as Guo et al. [120] and WhiteBox [121], aim to enhance transparency
and decision traceability in pruning decisions by aligning them with interpretable
features or visualization-based insights.
In summary, how to prune lies at the core of pruning algorithm design. Each strategy reflects a different view on parameter importance and model redundancy, and many methods
– 18 –
Background and Related Works
may fall into multiple categories.
How to configure pruning. Pruning configuration refers to the process of determining
how much to prune at different levels of a neural network—globally or layer-wise—by assigning appropriate sparsity levels. In structured pruning, this typically involves deciding
how many channels, filters, or blocks to remove from each layer. Pruning configuration is
crucial because it directly affects the resulting model’s accuracy, efficiency, and hardware
compatibility. A poor configuration may either under-prune (yielding negligible gains) or
over-prune (leading to accuracy degradation), even if the pruning criterion itself is well
designed. Broadly, there are two main approaches to pruning configuration: manual [65],
[82], [88], [89], [122] and automatic [50], [59], [63], [90], [95], [97], [102], [107], [109],
[113], [114], [115], [116], [119], [120]. Manual configuration involves explicitly defining
the sparsity ratio for each layer, often guided by heuristics, prior experience, or exhaustive
trial-and-error. This approach offers full control and transparency, making it suitable for
small networks or well-understood tasks. However, it is labor-intensive and lacks adaptability. Manually designed configurations are usually tailored to specific architectures and
datasets, which limits their transferability and scalability [63]. Automatic configuration
aims to determine the pruning configuration adaptively, with minimal or no human intervention [115]. These methods optimize pruning rates to meet global constraints—such as
target MACs, parameter budgets, latency, or energy consumption—while preserving accuracy. Techniques span a variety of learning and optimization strategies, including reinforcement learning [113], [116], [123], evolutionary search [110], [115], lasso regression [95],
[109], differentiable pruning [107], optimization [93], [108], [119], and meta-learning [63].
Automatic methods significantly improve scalability and adaptivity, making them particularly valuable in large-scale or real-world deployment scenarios [114]. In summary, pruning
configuration plays a central role in balancing model efficiency and accuracy. While manual approaches offer simplicity and insight, automatic strategies provide robustness and
scalability, making them essential for modern pruning pipelines.
When to prune. Pruning may be applied at different stages of the model lifecycle: before training, during training, or after training. Pre-training pruning initializes sparse subnetworks prior to training, often relying on random or heuristic criteria [59], [63], [110],
[122]. While efficient, this approach risks discarding important parameters prematurely.
Training-aware pruning integrates pruning decisions into the training process, enabling
the model to adapt to sparsity gradually [65], [113], [121], [124]. Although more effective
in preserving accuracy, it often complicates the training pipeline and increases computational cost. Post-hoc pruning, in contrast, operates on a fully trained model and removes
redundant or less important components based on their learned representations. This decouples pruning from the training process, making it simpler to implement and more flexible across architectures and datasets [81]. Importantly, post-hoc pruning allows the use
of sophisticated pruning criteria (e.g., based on spectral properties or sensitivity analysis)
that require access to a well-trained model [80]. In this thesis, we adopt post-hoc pruning
due to its modularity, reproducibility, and compatibility with off-the-shelf pre-trained models. This approach enables us to focus on developing effective pruning strategies (e.g., filter
importance estimation) without being constrained by the dynamics of training. Moreover,
post-hoc pruning facilitates fair and transparent benchmarking, as the same pre-trained
model can be used across different pruning methods.
– 19 –
Chapter 2
How often to prune. Pruning can be done in a one-shot fashion, where the pruning step is
applied once to a trained model, followed by fine-tuning to recover accuracy. This approach
is simple and efficient, making it suitable for rapid prototyping and low-cost deployment
[96]. However, it may be suboptimal in terms of performance preservation, especially when
aggressive pruning is applied. Alternatively, iterative pruning alternates between pruning
and retraining over several rounds. This gradual strategy allows the model to adapt to the
evolving sparsity pattern, often resulting in better convergence and higher final accuracy
[99]. Although more computationally expensive, iterative pruning can produce more compact and better-performing models, particularly when high compression ratios are desired.
In this thesis, we adopt both strategies depending on the experimental context. One-shot
pruning is used in SLIMING to evaluate pruning criteria and ensure fair comparison with
baseline methods. Iterative pruning is employed in CORING to fully exploit the potential of the proposed method, particularly when aiming for high compression with minimal
accuracy degradation.
Pruning is often combined with other compression techniques, such as quantization,
NAS, tensor decomposition, or knowledge distillation, for improved efficiency. Despite
its conceptual simplicity, pruning remains one of the most versatile and widely adopted
approaches in model compression. For an in-depth survey on pruning, we refer the reader
to [6].
2.2.2
Low-Rank Approximations
The weights of DNNs, also called parameters, are inherently structured as matrices or
higher-order tensors. The primary operations in DNNs—matrix multiplication and convolution—are applied to tensor data as it moves through the network, accounting for a significant portion of the computational load. As a result, low-rank approximations emerge
as an effective approach for reducing both the number of parameters and the computational complexity of DNNs. This technique leverages the low-rank nature of the model’s
weights, factorizing them into smaller matrices or tensors using either matrix or tensor decompositions. In the following, we review decomposition methods tailored to two popular
architectures: CNN, and Transformer.
Convolutional Neural Networks
Existing low-rank decomposition methods [3], [9], [15], [18], [46], [125], [126], [127] typically involve three main steps. First, the weight tensor W is factorized into smaller components, such as factor matrices [9], [15], [18] or core tensors [3], [125], [126]. Next, the original convolution operation is replaced with new operators, for instance, lower-dimensional
convolutions with reduced complexity [3], [9], [15], [18] or tensor multiplications [125],
[126], derived from the factorized representation. Finally, either the network is fine-tuned
to recover accuracy [3], [15], [18], or the compact model is trained from scratch [9], [125],
[126]. We categorize existing works based on the factorization step, as it represents the key
differentiating factor.
– 20 –
Background and Related Works
The most widely adopted and foundational approach to CNN compression using lowrank matrix approximations is based on SVD [7], [9], [10], [11], [12], [13], [14], [15], [16],
[24], [128], [129]. Pioneering approaches [14], [15], [16] apply truncated SVD, where the
2
weight tensor W are flattened into a 2-order matrix W ∈ RO×ID . Given a truncated rank
R, W is factorized into orthogonal and diagonal matrices, which are then used to replace
the original layer with two new sequential convolutional layers of size R × I × D × D
and O × R × 1 × 1. Zhang et al. [15] use the product of the principal component analysis
energy of all layers to determine the truncated rank. Idelbayev et al. [13] formulate the
rank selection as a combinatorial optimization problem and proposed to solve it with SGD
during the training process. In ALDS [12], Liebenwein et al. propose an automatic layerwise decomposition selector using the Eckart–Young–Mirsky theorem. BSR [11] employs
a modified beam search that can perform a joint optimization of the rank allocations over
all the layers. TRP [128] integrates low-rank approximations and regularization into the
training process. SVD was combined with knowledge distillation in LRDKT [14], or with
pruning in CC [7], leveraging the advantages of both approaches. PENNI [129] decomposes
parameters into tiny sets of basis kernels and accompanying coefficient matrices. LRPET [9]
trains low-rank compressed networks from scratch, alternately performing SGD training
and projection of each weight matrix onto the corresponding low-rank manifold. Bi-JSVD
[24] jointly decomposes similar layers, projecting weights with the same structures into the
same subspace.
A natural progression from low-rank matrix approximations to higher-order tensor decomposition methods was seen with the introduction of the CPD in CNN compression [17],
[18], [130]. Lebedev et al. [18] pioneered this approach by factorizing the 4-order weight
tensor W ∈ RO×I×D×D into a sum of rank-1 tensors, expressed as W = [[A, B, C, D]],
where A, B, C, and D are factor matrices of size O×R, I×R, D×R and D×R, respectively,
and R is the CPD rank. These matrices are used to replace the original layer with four new
sequential convolutional layers. Although this method preserves the multidimensional nature of the weight tensor, it processes the tensor at a coarse level, opening up the potential
for a more fine-grained treatment. Subsequent CPD-based approaches [17] proposes to ref ∈ RO×I×D2 with the argument that the kernel size is small

Although this approach [17] simplifies the tensor structure, it compromises the multidimensionality of the weight tensor, potentially causing information loss—particularly in modern
architectures that use larger kernel sizes. In Chapter 5, we introduce NORTON, a filter decomposition method. This approach was built on the fundamental insight that stems from
the convolution layer’s nature, where the input undergoes convolution independently with
each filter, and the resulting outputs are aggregated to generate the final feature map. In
NORTON, the layer’s 4-order weight is interpreted as a set of 3-order filters, and each is
then factorized individually.
Around the same time that the CPD was introduced, the TD also made its way into CNN
compression, first applied by Kim et al. [3]. In this latter, the authors decompose the weight
tensor W into a core tensor C ∈ RO×I×D×D and two factor matrices U(1) ∈ RO×R1 and
U(2) ∈ RI×R2 , given the multilinear rank-(R1 , R2 ). U(2) , C, and U(1) are sequentially used

to replace the original layer with three new convolutional layers of size R2 × I × 1 × 1,

work include [19], [20], [21], [62], [131], [132], [133]. Liu et al. [21] propose an alternating
optimization algorithm that iteratively solves a network training sub-problem and a TD
sub-problem to compress the network with performance assurance. Yin et al. [132] propose
BATUDE, a budget-aware TD-based approach that estimates the ranks during training.
Dai et al. [19] present a dual-model training strategy with an iterative and adaptive rank
reduction. TD is jointly used with other compression methods such as NAS in HALOC [62]
or pruning in [131]. Gabor et al. [133] point out that TD is often associated with a relatively
large core tensor, leading to an increased number of parameters that need to be stored. To
mitigate this, they propose the use of hierarchical TD for CNN compression, where the
fourth-order core tensor is further decomposed into smaller 3-order core tensors.
As a generalized model that combines CPD and TD, BTD has gained attention for its
application in CNN compression [134], [135]. Wang et al. [134] employ BTD by first ref ∈ RO×I×D2 and then factorizing it into a core tensor

by enforcing identical core tensors. To address the numerical instability arising from this
shared core structure, they propose an alternating sensitivity correction method, ensuring
the network remains robust while achieving better approximation.

Additional contributions in this domain include [22], [23], [64], [137], [138], [139]. For

for DNNs. Yin et al. [137] formulate TTD-based model compression as an optimization
problem with constraints on tensor ranks, and then iteratively solved it with the ADMM.
In HODEC [138], Yin et al. analyze the underlying reason and mitigation strategy for the
inefficiency of the classical TTD-format convolution. Recently, Sui et al. [22] introduced
CEPD, co-exploring structured sparsification and TTD. Ma et al. [139] introduce a fully
tensorized ConvLSTM framework for hyperspectral image classification, in which all convolutional and recurrent weight tensors are jointly compressed in the tensor train format
via an efficient tensor-sequenced convolution.

Nets, where the layers of a DNN were compressed through tensor ring factorization. In
this method, 4-order weight tensor W ∈ RO×I×D×D was decomposed by neglecting the
dimensions associated with the kernel, resulting in three smaller factor tensors U (1) ∈
RR1 ×R2 ×D×D , U (2) ∈ RR3 ×O×R2 , and U (3) ∈ RR1 ×I×R3 , where (R1 , R2 , R3 ) is the tensor
