Figure 5.1: Comparison of tensor decompositions approaches.
Although this method preserves the multidimensional nature of the weight tensor, it processes the tensor at a coarse level. This opens up the potential for a more fine-grained
treatment. In contrast, in [17] the authors suggested reshaping the 4-order weight tensor
into a 3-order tensor, followed by CPD to obtain 3 factor matrices and 3 sublayers. The
authors argued that the kernel size is relatively small, so it can be ignored. However, this
resizing process compromises the multidimensionality of the weight tensor, resulting in information loss during approximation. Moreover, this argument does not seem reasonable
with recent modern architectures where the kernel size increases significantly [5].
Our proposed method operates on the original weight tensor in a filter-by-filter manner.
The key insight lies in the fact that in a convolution layer, the input undergoes convolution separately with each filter, and the outputs are then aggregated to generate the final
feature map. Therefore, it is intuitive to decompose each 3-order filter tensor individually. The filters decomposition approach offers a higher level of granularity compared to
its counterpart, layer decomposition. With filters decomposition, not only the multidimensional property is strictly preserved, but also the layer’s 4-order weight is spontaneously
interpreted as a set of 3-order filters. Additionally, an interesting side effect of filters decomposition is that it leads to a narrower range of ranks, which simplifies the rank selection
process, as demonstrated later in Subsection 5.2.1. Another notable difference is that filters decomposition replaces the original layer with 3 sublayers, while layer decomposition
requires 4 sublayers, potentially increasing network depth unnecessarily and introducing
the possibility of gradient vanishing issues. It is worth mentioning that the distinction between layer decomposition and filters decomposition aligns with a similar categorization
seen in network pruning, specifically filter pruning and weight pruning, emphasizing the
fine-grained granularity aspect.
The second consideration of this work is to combine filters decomposition and filter
pruning to leverage the independent advantages of each method. While previous studies
– 67 –
Chapter 5
[8], [160], [182] have used low-rank representations and network pruning for compression
purposes, they have not explored an orthogonal combination of these techniques. In these
works, low-rank representations have been used only in the pruning step without directly
contributing to the reduction of model size. In contrast, our approach takes a different
direction by sequentially applying low-rank representations and pruning in two separate
phases, enabling a dual compression process. To the best of our knowledge, this direction
has not been extensively investigated in the literature.
There are two possible arrangements for the combination: decomposing then pruning,
and pruning then decomposing. Previous works [131], [182] have adopted the pruning then
decomposing scheme, utilizing the Taylor expansion-based pruning criterion [104] and the
TD [67]. However, this work [182] lacks comprehensive analysis and experiments, leaving
room for further investigation. To address this gap, our study explores the decomposing
then pruning scheme, where the model is first decomposed using CPD and then subjected
to structured pruning. We argue that this order presents greater challenges compared to its
counterpart, as the model’s architecture becomes more complex after decomposition, with
certain constraints imposed on the sublayers. To adapt to the decomposed components,
we propose to use the PABS [183] as a suitable filter pruning metric, which will be further
discussed in Subsection 5.2.2.
Briefly, the contributions of this work are three-fold:
• Firstly, we introduce a novel filters decomposition method, promoting its differentiation with existing layer decomposition and reshaped decomposition methods.
• Secondly, we investigate the sequential combination of filters decomposition and filter pruning. We propose a novel filter pruning algorithm designed to address the
challenges associated with this integration scheme.
• Thirdly, we evaluate the proposed framework on representative vision tasks including image classification, object detection, instance segmentation, and keypoint detection. We compare NORTON with SOTA methods in both low-rank representations
and structured pruning domains to demonstrate the superiority of our method. Notably, our model with different compression levels can consistently outperform prior
arts (see Figure 5.5).
5.2
The NORTON Framework
In this section, we present the pipeline of our approach, which comprises two main phases:
decomposition and pruning, as illustrated in Figure 5.2. Firstly, the original model is decomposed into CPDBlocks (as explained in Subsection 5.2.1). Next, the decomposed CPDBlocks
undergo the filter pruning algorithm (as described in 5.2.2). This algorithm selectively removes filters from the CPDBlocks based on certain criteria, effectively reducing the model’s
computational and memory requirements. Finally, a fine-tuning process is conducted to
refine the compact model. Empirically, only one process of fine-tuning as presented in
our proposal is sufficient instead of considering it after every phase of decomposition and
– 68 –
Enhanced Network Compression Through Tensor Decompositions and Pruning
pruning. This choice simplifies the methodology, reduces computational requirements, and
maintains satisfactory performance. This scheme is applied simultaneously to all convolution layers of the original model. However, for the sake of simplicity, subsections 5.2.1 and
5.2.2 will focus on discussing one layer, as shown in Figure 5.3.

Filters Decomposition Using the CPD
Based on (2.16) and the CPD definition in (2.5), we can apply the CPD to each individual
filter W i in order to obtain a compact representation. By decomposing W i using CPD, we

By substituting (5.1) into (2.16), we obtain a new CPD-based approach to compute the
convolution. This approach involves a sequence of mappings using the factor matrices
instead of high-order tensors. The resulting equation for this CPD-based convolution is:
O i (x, y) =

Starting from (5.2), we observe that the CPD-based convolution involves element-wise multiplications between the input tensor I and the factor matrices Ai , Bi , and Ci . It is important to note that the order of the convolutions can be rearranged without affecting the
final result. This flexibility allows us to describe the computation as a sequential block of
convolutions with smaller kernels, followed by a summation. First, the input tensor I can
be convolved with the kernel Ai along the input channel dimension. The resulting tensor
is then convolved with the kernel Bi along the spatial dimensions. Finally, the output of
the second convolution is convolved with the kernel Ci along the spatial dimensions. The
outputs of these convolutions are summed up to compute the final output tensor O i . The
following set of equations captures these operations in a concise manner:
X i (x + m, y + n, r) =
Y i (x, y + n, r) =
Z i (x, y, r) =
O i (x, y) =

(5.5)

where X i ∈ RHin ×Win ×R , Y i ∈ RHout ×Win ×R , and Z i ∈ RHout ×Wout ×R .
One should note that equations (5.3), (5.4), and (5.5) can be seen as convolutions and
can be easily implemented using common deep learning frameworks. Specifically, equation (5.3) can be computed using a classical two-dimensional convolution operation, while
equations (5.4) and (5.5) can be computed via group convolutions. One can refer to Figure
5.4, which illustrates the structure of the CPDBlock. To ensure compatibility with classical
frameworks, certain basic preprocessing operations, including reshaping and mode permutations, are required for adapting the kernel. Specifically, for the O factors Ai of dimensions
I × R, they need to be reshaped into a kernel of size 1 × 1 × I × (R · O). This enables
the application of a classical two-dimensional convolution for equation (5.3). Additionally,
for the group convolutions in equations (5.4) and (5.5), the kernels should be remodeled as
1 × D × 1 × (R · O) and D × 1 × 1 × (R · O), respectively. These modifications ensure the
appropriate computation of Y and Z. By performing these preprocessing steps, the CPDbased convolutions can be seamlessly integrated into existing deep learning frameworks.

About rank and the compression. The choice of rank R in CPD plays a crucial role in
balancing model compression and accuracy. Kruskal’s theory provides a weak upper bound
on the maximum rank [67], expressed as:

R ≤ min I · D, D2 .
(5.7)
This upper bound offers a guideline for selecting an appropriate rank, ensuring a reasonable
trade-off between model compression and preservation of critical features. By fixing a rank
R, the CPDBlock achieves a significant reduction in the number of parameters compared to
the original layer, which consists of O·I ·D2 parameters. Through the CPD-based approach,
NORTON achieves a compact representation of the filters, resulting in a reduced parameter
count on the order of O · R · (I + 2D). The computational complexity of the original layer
is O (I · O · D2 · H · W ). In contrast, the three sub-layers exhibit complexities of O(I · O ·
R · H · W ), O (O · R · D · H · W ), and O (O · R · D · H · W ), respectively. As a result, the
CPDBlock significantly reduces the computational load to O (R · O · (I + 2D) · H · W ).
It’s important to note that the rank constraint introduced in (5.7) guarantees a favorable
reduction in both the number of parameters and computational complexity.
5.2.2
CPDBlock Pruning
After demonstrating the use of filter decomposition and its integration into the new architecture, we will now delve into the methodology of factor matrices pruning. This technique
focuses on reducing the number of parameters by pruning the factor matrices obtained
through the decomposition process.
First, it is important to note that each output O k is computed based on three factor
matrices: Ak , Bk , and Ck . Therefore, when pruning the kernel related to a specific output,
all three matrices must be considered for removal. This necessitates the use of a pruning
criterion that takes into account the interdependencies among the three matrices. Second, it
is worth mentioning that when the CPD is unique, it is unique up to scaling and permutation
ambiguities. In other words, if two 3-order filters, W i and W j , are strictly similar and
satisfy the uniqueness conditions of the CPD [67], then


 W i = [[Ai , Bi , Ci ]],
Ai = Aj ,
W j = [[Aj , Bj , Cj ]], ⇏ Bi = Bj ,
(5.8)


Wi =
Wj.
Ci = Cj .
– 71 –
Chapter 5
Instead, we have
Ai = Aj ΠΛA , Bi = Bj ΠΛB , and Ci = Cj ΠΛC ,
where Π is a permutation matrix, and the diagonal scaling matrices verify ΛA ΛB ΛC = I.
For all the aforementioned reasons, we have decided to choose the PABS [183] as a metric
to measure the distance between two CPDs. The use of the PABS distance measure is justified in both the unique and non-unique cases of CPD. In the unique case, PABS allows for
capturing distances between factor matrices, enabling the identification of redundant filters
based on their distance patterns, while handling scaling and permutation ambiguities. Let
ϕ(., .) be a function that computes the PABS between two factor matrices. If we reconsider
the example in (5.8) in the case of unique CPDs, we have


 W i = [[Ai , Bi , Ci ]],
ϕ(Ai , Aj ) = 0,
W j = [[Aj , Bj , Cj ]], ⇒ ϕ(Bi , Bj ) = 0,
(5.9)


Wi =
Wj.
ϕ(Ci , Cj ) = 0.
Even in the non-unique cases, PABS can still be effective in identifying redundancies, as
it captures the distance between different sets of factor matrices representing the same
tensor as will be confirmed in the simulations. This enables the pruning process to remove
filters that contribute minimally to model performance or exhibit high similarity to other
filters, resulting in a more compact model while preserving critical features and maintaining
performance.
The core idea of CPDBlock pruning is to construct a distance matrix D, where each element Dij corresponds to the distance between the factor matrices [[Ai , Bi , Ci ]] and [[Aj , Bj , Cj ]].
The pruning process involves iteratively identifying the pair of decompositions, i and j,
corresponding to the minimum value of Dij , and removing one of them. The removed decomposition in our strategy between i and j is the one that most closely resembles the rest
of the decompositions, ensuring that the pruned one retains the representation that is most
similar to the remaining ones. The distance matrix D ∈ RO×O can be expressed as
B
C
Dij = αDA
ij + βDij + γDij ,
(5.10)
B
C
where DA
ij = ϕ(Ai , Aj ) (similarly for Dij and Dij ), and α, β and γ are weight parameters,
whose sum is equal to 1. The pruning strategy is outlined in Algorithm 5, which is complemented by a visual representation in the right half of Figure 5.3. The complexity of the
CPDBlock Pruning algorithm is O (O2 · (O − N )), resulting from pairwise comparisons in
the calculation of the distance matrix and subsequent iterations for factor deletion.

Experiments
5.3.1
Experimental Setup
Architectures and Datasets. In order to demonstrate the adaptability of NORTON, we
assess three representative architectures: VGG-16 [42], ResNet-56/110 with residual blocks
– 72 –

[38], and DenseNet-40 with dense blocks [44]. These models are tested on the CIFAR-10
dataset [53]. Additionally, to validate the scalability of NORTON, experiments are conducted on the challenging ImageNet dataset [36] using the ResNet-18/50 architecture. Configurations. On CIFAR [53], fine-tuning was carried out for 400 epochs with a batch
size of 256, momentum of 0.9, weight decay of 0.0005, and an initial learning rate of 0.01.
On ImageNet [36], fine-tuning was conducted for 200 epochs with a batch size of 128, a
momentum of 0.99, a weight decay of 0.0001, and a cosine learning rate scheduler with
an initial learning rate of 0.1. On COCO [41], models are fine-tuned following the default
recipe of Torchvision [37]. In this work, we choose the weight parameters to compute the
distance in (5.10) as α = β = γ = 13 .

while reducing much more computation costs and enjoying a similar number of parameters.

88% of MACs and 87% of parameters with almost no loss, just 0.12%; or 96% MACs and 98%

subject it to an ultra-high compression rate of 99% reduction in both MACs and parameters,
and the proposed method remains firm, yielding a modest loss. These results are illustrated

measure the approximation error between the original model and the decomposed model,
we employ the NMSE defined as:

Chapter 5
Table 5.5 shows the complexity reduction, approximation error, and accuracy before
and after fine-tuning. Obviously, without fine-tuning, the higher the rank is, the better
the weights are approximated, but with less compression obtained. With 5 ≤ R, our filter
decomposition method produces comparable accuracies (maximum 1.46% drop) with the
original model without fine-tuning. This suggests that our decomposition step behaves
well without fine-tuning. In addition, we demonstrate that our decomposition step can
effectively work when followed by a fine-tuning step. The results show that after finetuning, the accuracy is completely restored for all cases. However, it should be noted that
the achieved compression ratios are not as favorable as those obtained with the proposed
hybrid strategy.
Notably, our proposed method has not met the degeneracy problem (i.e., instability
issue when training a CNN with decomposed layers in the CPD format) as in a reshaped
decomposition approach [17]. We suspect that the filter decomposition method is more
fine-grained than the reshaped one. The trend of NMSE demonstrates a strong correlation
between the approximation error and the accuracy without fine-tuning.
5.4.2
The Effect of the Rank and the Pruning Ratio Selection
To evaluate the effect of pruning ratios and rank on compression and accuracy, we conducted additional simulations depicted in Figure 5.6. These simulations involve three compression levels, namely None (N), Soft (S), Moderate (M), and Hard (H) pruning ratios. Each
level represents a different degree of pruning, allowing us to examine the impact of different
compression settings on the decomposed models.

These experiments provide a comprehensive understanding of how the selection of rank
and pruning ratio impacts complexity reduction and model performance. With a predefined complexity reduction goal, there are multiple choices for the rank and pruning ratio,
resulting in different outcomes. For example, to eliminate about 65% of MACs, as shown in
Figure 5.6, possible choices are 3N, 4S, 6M, and 8H which produce 94.18%, 93.67%, 92.77%,
and 93.15% accuracy after fine-tuning, respectively. Notably, these combinations, despite
– 78 –
Enhanced Network Compression Through Tensor Decompositions and Pruning
similar MACs reductions, lead to different levels of parameter reduction: 64.91%, 68.33%,
78.34%, and 87.49%, respectively. This observation raises questions about the optimal combination of rank and pruning ratio, warranting further investigation, particularly for hybrid
compression approaches.
5.4.3
Component Analysis
Given that NORTON operates as a hybrid method, a detailed examination of the efficacy of
its individual components becomes imperative. We conducted an experiment on the VGG16/CIFAR-10, focusing on the accuracy vs. MACs reduction Pareto curves of six approaches.
These approaches were organized into three direct comparisons: our decomposition part
versus Tucker decomposition [3], our pruning part versus Taylor pruning [104], and our
hybrid approach versus the combination of Taylor pruning and TD [182]. It’s worth noting
that we selected these approaches based on their relevance to our work, especially [182],
and their components (TD and Taylor pruning) being representative in network compression. Due to the absence of results for this architecture in the referenced papers, we reproduced the results for Taylor pruning [104], TD [3], and their combination [182]. The
outcomes are presented in Figure 5.7.
Effectiveness: In terms of pairwise comparisons, our components consistently outperform
their counterparts from [182]. For instance, our CPD consistently achieves higher performance than TD at similar compression ratios. Additionally, CPD demonstrates greater stability than TD, meaning its accuracy does not drop significantly as the CR increases (up
to 88%, see Table 5.5). TD, a layer decomposition approach, experiences a rapid loss of
performance beyond a 50% compression ratio, suggesting potential information loss due to
its neglect of spatial dimensions of the kernel (3rd and 4th modes). This comparison underscores the effectiveness of our proposed filter decomposition method compared to layer
decomposition and reshaped-based decomposition approaches. The subspace-based pruning method proves more effective and efficient than the Taylor pruning method. However,
both pruning methods experience a significant performance drop at a 70% compression
ratio threshold. Notably, the combination of Taylor pruning and TD exhibits suboptimal
performance, even worse than its individual components at many points. In contrast, NORTON effectively synthesizes the strengths of its components, yielding higher accuracy at
various points and achieving higher compression (beyond 90%). This experiment not only
strengthens the motivation of our hybrid approach but also reaffirms that our decomposition/pruning proposal can stand alone as a promising candidate for network compression.

Efficiency: Theoretical computational complexities for the proposed CPD and subspace
pruning were detailed in Section 5.2.2. Notably, the overheads associated with both the
decomposition and pruning stages are deemed insignificant, each requiring less than 10
seconds in our simulation. This is notably less than the time spent on the fine-tuning
step, which can extend up to 100 hours when using ResNet-50/ImageNet. Additionally, our
choice of a single-shot compression scheme ensures that the compression process occurs
only once. In contrast, the hybrid method in [182] employs an iterative scheme, incurring
higher costs as the decomposition and filters’ saliency estimation need recalculation in each
round. It’s essential to emphasize that the critical metric lies in the inference speed during the deployment of the compressed model, aptly represented by the reduction in MACs,
where our approach has proven to be particularly efficient.
This comprehensive evaluation shows that NORTON, as a hybrid approach, excels not
only in synthesizing the strengths of its individual components but also in outperforming
comparable methods in terms of accuracy and compression.
5.4.4
Feature Retention Across Compression Ratios
We qualitatively assess feature preservation in NORTON, complementing the established
efficiency from numerical results in Section 5.3. We randomly selected 5 images from the
ImageNet validation dataset and evaluated three compression levels for the original ResNet50 model: 50%, 64%, and 78%. Using GradCAM [172] for interpretation, we visualize and
analyze feature maps in the original and compressed models in Figure 5.8. It can be observed
that at different compression ratios, NORTON consistently proves robust in capturing and
preserving essential features across diverse classes. This robustness implies sustained effectiveness and reliability across varying scenarios and compression ratios, establishing
NORTON as a versatile choice for network compression.

5.5
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have introduced NORTON, a novel hybrid network compression method
that combines the strengths of tensor decompositions and structured pruning. Through extensive experiments and analyses, we have demonstrated the effectiveness and superiority
of NORTON in achieving significant model compression while preserving critical features
and maintaining performance. By leveraging filter decomposition and the integration of
structured pruning, NORTON offers a powerful approach for reducing model complexity
and the number of parameters, pushing the limits of network compression. Our results
have shown that NORTON outperforms existing SOTA compression techniques in terms of
compression ratios and accuracy retention. The combination of tensor decompositions and
structured pruning in NORTON provides a versatile framework that allows for fine-grained
control over the compression process. Moreover, NORTON’s ability to handle scaling and
permutation ambiguities further enhances its practicality and flexibility. The evaluation of
NORTON on various architectures and datasets has demonstrated its scalability and generalizability. Furthermore, we have analyzed the impact of rank and pruning ratios on the
compression and performance trade-offs, providing valuable insights for selecting optimal
configurations.
– 81 –
Chapter 6
Coupled Tensor Decomposition for
Compact Network Representation
In this chapter, we introduce an approach called coupled filters decomposition, which builds
on the key observation that redundancy exists among filters within a convolutional layer,
meaning that similar filters can produce partially overlapping outputs. Leveraging this
insight, we propose a joint decomposition of filters using coupled tensor decompositions,
specifically coupled CPD, which enables the sharing of a common factor matrix across similar filters. This joint factorization not only reduces the number of parameters but also
lowers computational complexity by eliminating redundant computations. To further enhance the efficiency of our approach, we first cluster the filters (prior to decomposition) into
groups that are better suited for coupled decomposition—where the coupling constraint is
less restrictive—using a custom metric based on the shared mode subspace. Extensive experiments across various architectures, datasets, and tasks validate the effectiveness of our
method, demonstrating its competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art model
compression techniques. Our code is available for research purposes at codec-ai.github.io.
6.1
Introduction
Among the existing paradigms for model compression, low-rank approximations emerge
as an effective approach to leverage the low-rank nature of the model’s weights by factorizing them into smaller factors. Based on the decomposition technique and its corresponding interpretation of the format of the weights, existing works can be categorized
into two branches: matrix decompositions and tensor decompositions. Matrix-based approaches [9], [10], [11], [12], [13], [14], [15], [16] usually flatten the 4-order weight tensor
into a 2-order matrix so that the SVD can be performed on it. However, this flattening can
disrupt the multidimensional nature of the weight tensor, potentially leading to information loss [22]. Tensor decomposition approaches such as CPD [17], [18], TD [3], [19], [20],
[21], and TTD [22] directly operate on the higher-order weight tensors, preserving their
multidimensional structure and providing a greater ability to capture complex, high-order
83
Chapter 6
information embedded in the parameters [20], [21], [143].

Figure 6.1: Comparison between standard convolution (top) and CoDeC-based convolution
with coupled CPD factors (bottom).
Coupled matrix or tensor decompositions extend the ideas of standard matrix and tensor decompositions to jointly analyze and factorize multiple related matrices or tensors [5].
These methods are especially useful when data comes from different but related sources or
modalities, where the goal is to exploit the underlying shared structure. The coupled matrix/tensors factorizations task requires one or more common dimensions among the matrices/tensors, and uses these similar dimensions to couple the decompositions with methods
such as alternating optimization [67]. Coupled matrix/tensor decompositions have proved
to be effective in various applications such as neuroimaging and data fusion [5]. For CNN
compression, coupled TTD is first adopted in [23] while coupled SVD is recently employed
in [24]. In these works [23], [24], the weights of layers with the same shape are simultaneously factorized. While reducing the number of parameters, this coupled layer decomposition scheme does not fully benefit from computational complexity reduction because the
inputs of these separate layers are different.
In this work, we highlight a key observation that drives our approach: within a convolutional layer, redundancy exists among the filters, as noted in various CNN compression
studies [187], particularly in similarity-based filter pruning methods. Since all filters extract
information from a common input, partially similar filters may produce partially similar
output features. To enhance computational efficiency, the redundant computation of these
similar parts should be avoided. For example, in the top half of Figure 6.1, the filters W 1
and W 3 exhibit partial similarity, leading to their output feature maps O 1 and O 3 sharing a
similar component (shown in blue) that is computed twice, causing redundant calculations.
To avoid such duplicative computations and enhance efficiency, these filters can be jointly
decomposed.
Building on these insights, we introduce the concept of coupled filters decomposition.
In this scheme, multiple filters are jointly approximated using coupled tensor decompositions. To demonstrate the use of this method, we employ coupled CPD as a representative
– 84 –
Coupled Tensor Decomposition for Compact Network Representation
example due to its simplicity and efficiency, although our approach can be adapted to other
decomposition techniques. Specifically, instead of decomposing each filter individually, we
propose jointly factorizing them along a specific mode. After decomposition, the jointly decomposed filters share a common factor matrix in the selected mode while retaining their
unique factor matrices in other modes. This approach suggests that filters possess both
common and particular characteristics. For instance, in the bottom of Figure 6.1, the two
similar filters W 1 and W 3 are jointly factorized along the first mode, yielding a common
factor matrix A(1) and different factor matrices in the other modes, namely matrices B1 ,
C1 , and B3 , C3 . Notably, since these filters share the same input tensor, the computation
between the decomposed common factor and the input must only be performed once.
A critical aspect of the coupled filter decomposition scheme is choosing how many
and which filters to jointly decompose. Simply coupling all the filters in a layer presents
two challenges. First, only some filters may be similar, rather than all. Second, when the
number of filters is large, coupling all of them can result in excessive compression, which
may negatively impact accuracy. To address these issues, we introduce a clustering step
as a preprocessing phase, where filters within the same cluster exhibit greater similarity in
the joint mode subspace compared to those in other clusters. The goal of clustering is to
group filters that are similar in this subspace, enabling more effective joint decomposition.
Furthermore, by adjusting the number of clusters, we can more easily balance the tradeoff between compression rate and accuracy, making our framework adaptable to varying
requirements. We employ the K-means clustering algorithm for this purpose. Additionally,
to better align with the context of coupled CPD, we introduce a custom distance metric that
replaces the standard Euclidean distance. This proposed distance is based on the projection
of filters onto the joint mode subspace, thereby enhancing the performance of the coupled
decomposition.
The proposed approach distinguishes itself from existing low-rank approximations methods in several key aspects. Unlike the filter decomposition method in, where CPD is applied
separately to each filter, our coupled decomposition leverages shared factors across filters,
resulting in greater reductions in both the number of parameters and computational cost
for the same rank setting. In contrast to reshaped-based approaches [9], [10], [11], [12],
[13], [14], [15], [16], [17], [22], [126], [137], [143], CoDeC avoids any reshaping operations
by directly factorizing filters using CPD, thereby preserving the inherent multidimensional
structure of the convolutional weights. Moreover, compared to layer decomposition methods [17], [18], our approach introduces fewer intermediate layers, which helps alleviate
the gradient vanishing problem commonly associated with deep networks. The pioneering
work on layer-wise decomposition [18] employed CPD to exploit the redundancy in the
4-order weight tensor of convolutional layers. However, this approach was limited to compressing a single convolutional layer, whereas CoDeC is capable of compressing all layers
of a network simultaneously. In [3], Kim et al. applied TD in conjunction with variational
Bayesian matrix factorization for automatic rank selection, enabling a one-shot compression of the entire network. Nonetheless, their method only decomposes the first and second
modes of the weight tensor (corresponding to the output and input channels), while ignoring the third and fourth modes associated with the kernel dimensions. The authors justified
this by claiming that kernel sizes are typically small and can thus be neglected [3]. However, this simplification compromises the multidimensional structure of the weight tensor,
– 85 –
Chapter 6
potentially leading to information loss during approximation [22]. Moreover, this assumption does not hold for modern architectures, which often utilize larger kernel sizes. Further,
the work in [17] combines CPD and TD to address the issue of degeneracy (i.e., instability
or difficulty in fine-tuning the decomposition factors). In contrast, we did not encounter
degeneracy issues during the fine-tuning of CoDeC’s factorized layers.
Furthermore, compared to the coupled layers decomposition approach [23], [24], our
coupled filters decomposition approach offers a distinct advantage in computational efficiency. In the coupled layers decomposition approach, layers with identical shapes are
jointly factorized, but since each layer has its own input, the joint factor must be used
for each input. In contrast, our approach requires the joint factor to be computed with
the input only once, significantly reducing the number of computations. It is worth noting that the two approaches are of two levels of granularity, analogous to the distinction between weight pruning and filter pruning. From another perspective, CoDeC also
shares a common objective with structured pruning [59], [76], [79], [82], [111], [112],
[116], [117], [121], [124], [157], [188]: reducing redundancy among filters. The criterion used to evaluate filter importance forms the foundation of pruning techniques, which
can be broadly categorized into magnitude-based [79], [82], similarity-based [117], [184],
activation-based [121], regularization-based [59], [188], optimization-driven [116], [124],
and NAS-based approaches [111], [112]. However, CoDeC fundamentally differs in its
methodology. While pruning methods typically eliminate less important filters entirely,
CoDeC replaces each filter with a set of compact factor matrices. This distinction highlights CoDeC as a tensor decomposition approach rather than a pruning-based one. Finally,
it is worth noting that CoDeC is compatible with pruning or other compression techniques
such as quantization, NAS, and knowledge distillation, and can be seamlessly integrated
with them to achieve further efficiency gains.
Briefly, the contributions of this work are as follows:
• First, we propose CoDeC (Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation), a framework for CNN compression that consists of two key steps: (i) clustering filters based on a custom distance metric, and (ii) joint decomposition of similar
filters using the coupled CPD.
• Second, we demonstrate how this approach can be efficiently implemented in standard deep learning frameworks, ensuring its practical applicability with optimized
computation.
• Third, we evaluate the effectiveness of our approach across diverse vision tasks, including image classification, object detection, instance segmentation, and keypoint
detection. A comprehensive comparison with existing methods highlights the advantages of our method.

6.2
The CoDeC Framework
In this section, we introduce CoDeC, our proposed framework designed to address the redundancy among filters in CNNs. The central idea of CoDeC is to jointly factorize groups of
similar filters along a specific mode, rather than decomposing each filter independently. To
enable effective joint decomposition, the framework groups filters based on their similarity
in the chosen mode, ensuring that filters within the same group exhibit higher similarity
in the joint mode subspace compared to those in different groups. To achieve this, CoDeC
adopts a two-stage process, as illustrated in Figure 6.2, consisting of the following components:
1. Clustering: The filters of the original convolution layer are classified into K clusters
(Subsection 6.2.1) as illustrated in the left half of Figure 6.2.
2. Coupled decomposition: Filters within each cluster are jointly decomposed using
coupled CPD (Subsection 6.2.2). The decomposition processes across all K clusters
are depicted in the right half of Figure 6.2. Once all clusters are processed in parallel,
the output factor matrices are combined to form the CoDeCBlock (Subsection 6.2.3).
This scheme is simultaneously applied to all convolution layers of the original model. However, for the sake of simplicity, we will focus on discussing only one layer.
– 87 –
Chapter 6
6.2.1
Clustering
We propose to cluster the O filters into K separate clusters, where 1 ≤ K ≤ O. The k-th
cluster (1 ≤ k ≤ K) consists of ok filters. The total
PK number of filters across all clusters
equals the total number of filters of the layer, i.e., k=1 ok = O. The left half of Figure 6.2
illustrates a simple clustering process with O = 8, K = 3, o1 = 3, o2 = 2, and o3 = 3. Our
intuition is that grouping similar filters in a common cluster will improve the quality of the
coupled decomposition. To this end, we may consider any clustering method; however, we
employ the K-means clustering approach as it is simple and effective.
The objective of the clustering is to gather in the same cluster filters whose CPDs have
more or less the same mode-1 factor matrices. To this end, the intuitive idea would be to
decompose filters into CPDs and perform some sort of criteria on the mode-1 factors to
cluster the similar filters. However, this idea necessitates (i) to have the CPD of each filter
before the clustering, (ii) to find a criterion that is adapted to the CPD because the CPDs
of two similar filters, if unique, are not equivalent (the factor A for the first one will be
different from the factor A of the second one), due to the trivial scaling and permutation
ambiguities of the CPD. Regarding these two arguments, we propose an SVD-based strategy
as follows.
Let us consider two filters W i and W j , of the same size I × D × D, whose CPDs
can be coupled with respect to the mode-1 factor matrices, i.e., W i = [[Ai , Bi , Ci ]] and
W j = [[Aj , Bj , Cj ]], with Ai = Aj ΠΛ, where Π is a permutation matrix and Λ is a
diagonal scaling matrix. One should note that mode-1 unfoldings of these filters, of size
I × (D2 ), can be expressed as
unfold1 W i ≜ Ai (Ci ⊙ Bi )⊤ = Ui Σi Vi⊤ ,
(6.1)
unfold1 W j ≜ Aj (Cj ⊙ Bj )⊤ = Uj Σj Vj⊤ ,
(6.2)
SVD
SVD
where Ui ∈ RI×R , Σi ∈ RR×R , and Vi ∈ RR×(D·D) . This means that if I < R then
Ai and Ui (respectively Aj and Uj ) span the same range space. So finally, if W i and
W j can be coupled with respect to the mode-1 factor, the matrices Ui and Uj (retrieved
from their mode-1 unfoldings’ SVDs) span the same range space. This means that instead
of following the easy and natural (but computationally extensive) idea that we described
at first, one can detect potentially coupled filters using their left singular vector matrices.
In other words, we can say that using some convenient distance metric d(.), we can have
d (Ai , Aj ) = d (Ui , Uj ).
A natural way to quantify the similarity between Ui and Uj is to compare their orthogonal projectors, which capture the subspaces they span. Since two matrices span the same
range space if and only if their orthogonal projectors are identical, we define the following
distance metric:
(6.3)
d (Ui , Uj ) = Ui · U†i − Uj · U†j .
F
This measure ensures that the distance remains invariant to transformations within the
subspace, making it well-suited for comparing matrices whose columns may differ due to
basis choices but still describe the same underlying space. In our custom K-means algo– 88 –
Coupled Tensor Decomposition for Compact Network Representation
rithm, we use this projected distance to measure the distance between a filter and a centroid
in the assignment step, as well as to compute the inertia for an initialization.
To advocate for our proposed clustering approach, we give an experiment on synthetic
data of a convolution with O = I = 64, D = 3, R = 5, K = 4, o1 = 9, o2 = 16,
o3 = 25, o4 = 14. The ground truth factors are randomly generated, consisting of 4 matrices A ∈ R64×5 , 64 matrices B ∈ R3×5 , and 64 matrices C ∈ R3×5 . The filters are generated
from the CPDs of these factor matrices. We compare three approaches: random clustering,
K-means using Euclidean distance, and K-means using projected distance. After applying the coupled CPD, which will be described in subsection 6.2.2, with the true rank (so
that error depends only on clustering), we reconstruct the filters from the factor matrices.
We use the NMSE between the reconstructed filters and the original ones to evaluate the
quality of the decomposition. The results, shown in Table 6.1, indicate that using K-means
consistently results in a lower NMSE compared to the random approach. This supports
our intuition that clustering improves approximation quality. Additionally, the proposed
projected distance proves to be effective as it gives lower approximation errors compared
to the Euclidean distance in all cases. Especially, our method can find the ground-truth
clustering in the case of given ground-truth K, resulting in a negligible NMSE. This experiment shows that the proposed clustering approach works well with synthetic data. A more
comprehensive experiment on the impact of the clustering approaches will be discussed in
detail in subsection 6.4.1.

Starting from (6.6), we observe that the coupled CPD-based convolution involves elementwise multiplications between the input tensor I and the factor matrices A, Bi , and Ci .
This computation is described as a sequential block of convolutions with smaller kernels,
followed by a summation. First, the input tensor I can be convoluted with the kernel A
along the input channel dimension. The resulting tensor is then convoluted with the kernel
Bi along the spatial dimensions. Finally, the output of the second convolution is convoluted
with the kernel Ci along the spatial dimensions. The outputs of these convolutions are
summed up to compute the final output tensor O i . The following set of equations concisely

The order of computation in (6.6) is interchangeable, meaning that we can start by multiplying the input tensor I with the mode-2 or mode-3 factor matrices. However, beginning
with the first-mode factor matrix A is an optimal approach from the computational complexity perspective. As seen in (6.4), there are o outputs needed to be computed, which
would have to correspond to o intermediate outputs {X 1 , . . . , X o }. However, thanks to
the coupled decomposition concept, all the o filters share only one common factor matrix
A, thus, X 1 = . . . = X o = X . Only one operation is needed as shown in (6.7). This
obviously reduces o times the computational demand and also the memory footprint for
this intermediate calculation.

6.2.4
On the Choice of the Rank and the Number of Clusters
The number of clusters K and the rank R determine the compression ratio of the proposed decomposition scheme. The number of parameters of the original convolution layer
is O · I · D2 . For the CoDeCBlock, the number of parameters is R · (I · K + O · 2D).
The computational complexity of the original layer is O (O · I · D2 · H · W ), assuming
Hin = Hout = H, and Win = Wout = W for simplicity. For the CoDeCBlock, the computational complexity is O (R · (I · K + O · 2D) · H · W ). To ensure a positive reduction
in both the number of parameters and computational complexity, the following constraint
must be satisfied:
R · (I · K + O · 2D) < O · I · D2 .
(6.15)
Regarding the rank selection, unlike in the matrix case, where the singular value decomposition provides a rank-revealing decomposition, determining the rank of a tensor is known
to be an NP-hard problem [67]. In practice, the canonical polyadic rank is typically identified using numerical heuristics [5]. One widely used strategy is CORCONDIA [189] (CORe
CONsistency DIAgnostic), which evaluates the consistency of the estimated core tensor
with respect to an ideal identity tensor across different rank values, thereby helping to select a suitable rank. Such heuristics can also be applied to the coupled CPD case, although
they tend to be computationally expensive [5].
Moreover, in many application domains such as signal processing, chemometrics, or
biomedical engineering, the canonical polyadic rank often has physical interpretability: for
instance, it may represent the number of sources in direction of arrival estimation, the
number of fluorophores in fluorescence spectroscopy, or the number of propagation paths
in multiple-input and multiple-output communication systems. In such contexts, identifying the true rank is critical. However, in our setting, the goal is not source separation or
– 92 –
Coupled Tensor Decomposition for Compact Network Representation
physical modeling, but rather efficient network compression. The coupled CPD is leveraged
for its compactness, and in this context, the rank is not tied to any intrinsic or physically
meaningful quantity. Instead, a lower rank leads to a more compact model, making it a
design parameter that controls the trade-off between compression and performance.
It is worth emphasizing that in our approach, the CCPD rank selection is not arbitrary.
It is closely linked to the target CR. Specifically, given a desired CR, there is a mathematical
relationship between the CCPD rank R(l) and the number of clusters K (l) for each layer l,
given by:


As this equation shows, for a fixed compression ratio, only certain combinations of {R(l) , K (l) }Ll=1
are admissible. A simple yet practical strategy is to fix one variable (e.g., K (l) ) across layers
and solve for R(l) to meet the parameter budget. In our work, we follow this principle and
select R and K empirically to achieve a good trade-off between compression and performance. A more detailed analysis of the impact of this choice is provided in Subsection 6.4.2.
To further contextualize the selection of R and K, we provide a theoretical comparison
of parameter efficiency in Table 6.2, based on the analytical expressions of the number of
parameters for various low-rank decompositions. For simplicity, we assume identical rank
values across modes (e.g., R1 = R2 = · · · = R), to facilitate a fair and interpretable comparison. While each decomposition adopts its own notion of rank—often varying in typical
magnitude and interpretation—we can still derive qualitative insights from the expressions.
Among the compared approaches, the CPD of Lebedev [18] yields the most compact representation in terms of the number of parameters when the rank R is small. As shown
in Table 6.2, CoDeC is among the most compact decompositions. In Section 6.3, we will
demonstrate that this compactness is also accompanied by competitive accuracy.

6.3
Experiments
6.3.1
Experimental Setup
Architectures and Datasets: To demonstrate the adaptability of CoDeC, we assess several
architectures: VGG-16 [42], ResNet-20/32/56/110 with residual blocks [38], DenseNet-40
with dense blocks [44], and SqueezeNet with fire modules [60]. These models are tested on
the CIFAR-10/100 dataset. Additionally, to validate the scalability of CoDeC, experiments
are conducted on the ImageNet dataset [36] using ResNet-18/32/50/152 [38] architectures.
Furthermore, the compressed ResNet-50 model is employed as the backbone network for
FasterRCNN [73], MaskRCNN, and KeypointRCNN [74] on the COCO-2017 dataset [41].
Configuration: Fine-tuning uses a batch size of 128, momentum of 0.9, and weight
decay of 2e−5. For CIFAR, we employ a cosine learning rate scheduler with an initial rate
of 0.01 over 300 epochs; for ImageNet, the scheduler starts at 0.05 for 150 epochs. On
COCO, Faster/Mask-RCNN is fine-tuned for 26 epochs while KeypointRCNN is fine-tuned
for 46 epochs.
Baselines: CoDeC is compared with related works in decomposition [3], [7], [8], [9],
[11], [12], [13], [14], [15], [16], [17], [18], [19], [22], [23], [24], [62], [126], [128], [129], [131],
[132], [133], [135], [136], [137], [138], [140], [141], [156], [160], [161], pruning (P) [7], [8],
[22], [59], [76], [79], [82], [111], [112], [116], [117], [121], [124], [131], [157], [188], NAS (N)
[59], [62], [111], [112], [116], [140], [141], [155], [156], [160], [184], knowledge distillation
(K) [14], [59], [111], [157], [160], [161], and weight sharing (W) [23], [24], [129], [186], [188],
[190]. We adjusted the hyperparameters to align with other methods for a comparable
compression rate, facilitating an analysis of the trade-off between compression rate and
accuracy, and vice versa. We follow the evaluation protocols described in Subsection 3.3.1.
For brevity, only notable results and comparisons are reported in Subsection 6.3.2, while
the complete set of experiments is available in our publication [34].
6.3.2

6.4.1
On the Clustering Method
We extend the experiment in subsection 6.2.1 to analyze the impact of the employed custom K-means clustering method for preprocessing. Unlike synthetic data, the filters of
pre-trained models do not always conform to coupled CPD, and the ground truths for K
and R are unknown. Therefore, it is essential to try multiple combinations of these hyperparameters to assess the impact of the clustering approach. Specifically, we extract the
second convolutional layer from the VGG model whose weight is of size 64×64×3×3. We
consider some combinations of the number of cluster K and the rank R guided by (6.15).
The results, shown in Figure 6.4, align with the conclusion in subsection 6.2.1. Even without
knowing the ground truth K and R, the proposed cluster approach proves to be effective as
it gives lower approximation errors compared to the Euclidean distance in most scenarios,
particularly for lower-rank cases.

6.4.2
Component-Wise Analysis of the CoDeC Framework
To better understand the contribution of individual components in our proposed method,
we provide a detailed analysis based on experiments in Figure 6.5. We focus on three key
aspects: the trade-off between rank R and number of clusters K under a fixed compression
target, the role of fine-tuning, and the impact of the coupling mechanism.
Interaction between R and K. The decomposition parameters R and K jointly control
the trade-off between model complexity and performance. For a fixed compression ratio,
multiple combinations of (R, K) can satisfy the same complexity constraint. Our results
show that while these configurations yield similar parameter and MACs reductions, they
lead to slightly different final accuracies. This highlights the importance of selecting R

and K jointly rather than independently. In our setting, we observe that NMSE is a reliable proxy for this selection, and we suggest its use as a guide for choosing configurations
that optimize the accuracy-compression trade-off. While the accuracy may vary slightly
across (R, K) configurations satisfying the same compression target, our experiments indicate that these variations remain minor. This suggests that the compression ratio itself
is the primary driver of performance, and the (R, K) configuration acts more as a design
parameter to meet this target, rather than a critical hyperparameter requiring extensive
tuning.
Effect of fine-tuning. We also analyze the performance gap between the decomposed
model before and after fine-tuning. We observe that for moderate compression configurations (e.g., R ≥ 5, K/O ≥ 3/8), CoDeC yields reasonable accuracy even without retraining. Fine-tuning, however, consistently improves performance across all configurations
and allows the model to closely match or even outperform the original. This supports the
robustness of the decomposition and its usability in low-resource scenarios.
Impact of the coupling mechanism. The coupling strategy in CoDeC consists in grouping filters into clusters and enforcing shared factor matrices within each cluster. This approach reduces the number of parameters, but also imposes structural constraints on the
model. To evaluate the effect of coupling, we compare configurations with identical rank R
but varying numbers of clusters K. The extreme case K = O corresponds to a fully uncoupled CPD, where each filter is decomposed independently. Experimental results indicate
that, for fixed R, increasing the number of clusters K (i.e., reducing the coupling strength)
consistently improves both the approximation quality (lower NMSE) and the final accuracy. This suggests that coupling introduces a trade-off between model compactness and
expressive power.

1
Chapter 6
6.4.3
Compression Efficiency
To empirically analyze the compression efficiency, we conduct experiments comparing the
compression time of our method, CoDeC, with three other approaches, including WhiteBox [121], ABCPruner [112], and CORING. Table 6.6 presents the time cost (compression
and fine-tuning runtime) and compression results (top-1 accuracy, MACs reduction, and
parameters reduction) across two models (VGG-16 and ResNet-56). In our setting, compression time refers only to the clustering and decomposition steps, which are executed
once during the offline compression phase, before fine-tuning or deployment. Once the
CoDeC blocks are constructed, no additional computational overhead is introduced during
inference or fine-tuning. Although the CoDeC framework may involve a fine-tuning step
after compression, this occurs offline during training and has no effect on inference latency
at deployment. WhiteBox is a feature map-based pruning method requiring several training
epochs to evaluate channel importance, making it time-intensive. ABCPruner solves a combinatorial optimization problem via a meta-heuristic search, incurring high runtime costs.
CORING, a low-rank approximation method, performs multi-shot pruning and fine-tuning,
further increasing the total runtime. As shown in Table 6.6, CoDeC achieves comparable
or better compression performance while significantly reducing the overall compression
runtime compared to the other methods. These results highlight CoDeC’s practicality in
real-world scenarios where compression efficiency must be considered alongside compression effectiveness.

6.4.4
On the Hyperparameter Selection
While CoDeC effectively balances compression and performance, certain limitations remain. One challenge lies in the use of NMSE as a proxy for accuracy. Although NMSE
provides a useful approximation of reconstruction quality, its correlation with classification performance is not always straightforward. This is especially true when considering
the heterogeneous sensitivity of different layers or dataset-specific dynamics. Hence, relying solely on NMSE can, in some cases, lead to suboptimal configuration choices.
Another limitation concerns the layerwise selection of the decomposition parameters
(R, K). In the current approach, (R, K) values are chosen per layer based on local considerations. However, this strategy does not guarantee global optimality across the network.
– 100 –
Coupled Tensor Decomposition for Compact Network Representation
A given layer may tolerate a higher or lower compression depending on its relative importance, and interactions between layers can affect overall model performance. Addressing
this limitation would require a coordinated compression strategy that considers joint optimization across layers.
To tackle these limitations, future research may explore adaptive compression strategies informed by layer sensitivity analysis. Integrating lightweight NAS or reinforcement
learning could help automate the discovery of (R, K) configurations tailored to global objectives. Additionally, designing evaluation metrics that go beyond NMSE and better reflect
task-specific performance could lead to more informed and effective compression decisions.
6.5
Conclusion
We have presented a novel CNN compression technique using the coupled filters decomposition method. By exploiting the redundancy in convolutional layers, our approach employs coupled CPD to jointly factorize filters. This method preserves the multidimensional
integrity of the weights while significantly reducing both computational complexity and
the number of parameters, resulting in more efficient models. To tackle filter similarity, we
introduced a projected distance metric for the K-means clustering algorithm, specifically
designed for the coupled CPD framework. This clustering technique effectively groups similar filters within the joint mode subspace, enabling more precise and efficient joint decomposition. Extensive experiments across various architectures, datasets, and tasks confirm
the efficacy and utility of our proposed framework in network compression. Additionally,
we have analyzed the impact of hyperparameter configurations on the trade-offs between
compression and performance, offering valuable guidance for selecting optimal settings.
– 101 –
Chapter 7
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions
Using Zeroth and First-Order
Information
Approximating nonlinear functions is fundamental to a wide range of scientific and engineering problems, including neural network training and nonlinear system identification.
Multivariate function decoupling—an approach that expresses a multivariate function as a
linear combination of univariate functions applied to linear projections of the input—has
recently gained attention for enabling flexible activation functions and parameter-efficient
models. However, existing decoupling frameworks are limited to vector-valued functions,
preventing their application to modern neural architectures such as Transformers and multilayer perceptron-based networks that operate on matrix-valued data. In this chapter, we
address this limitation by formalizing the decoupling of matrix-valued multivariate functions as a coupled matrix-tensor factorization problem. We propose an alternating least
squares algorithm that jointly leverages both zeroth- and first-order information of the
function to extract decoupled representations. We then apply this framework to compress
deep neural networks with matrix-valued architectures. Our method is evaluated on four
representative models across seven benchmark datasets. The results demonstrate consistent gains in compression and accuracy over state-of-the-art methods. To the best of our
knowledge, this is the first work to extend function decoupling to the matrix-valued setting
and to validate its effectiveness in the context of neural network compression. Our code
will be available for research purposes at flex-ai.github.io.
7.1
Introduction
The problem of learning to imitate and approximate nonlinear functions is crucial for solving many scientific challenges, including nonlinear system identification and neural network learning. A prominent direction addressing this challenge is multivariate polynomial
function decoupling, initially proposed by Dreesen et al. [28], and further developed in [29],
103
Chapter 7
[30], [191]. This approach aims to decompose a set of multivariate real polynomials into
linear combinations of univariate polynomial functions in linear forms of the input variables. One of the key ideas is to use the first-order derivatives on the input-output map
(Jacobian matrices), which then can be stacked into a tensor whose CPD factors are linked
to the weights of linear combinations and the univariate polynomial functions.
In the context of neural network learning, function decoupling offers two main advantages. First, it enables the usage of trainable or flexible activation functions, where the
activation functions are expressed as a weighted sum of predefined basis functions. This
kind of parameterization allows the model to tailor its nonlinearity to the data, enhancing
its representational power and potentially improving performance [29], [54]. Second, the
decoupled form tends to be more efficient in terms of the number of parameters, compared
to the coupled form, to represent the relationship between the input and output [191].
Applications of this idea in neural networks have been explored in several works. Zniyed
et al. [29] interpreted a subnetwork of a pre-trained neural network—comprising one or
multiple layers—as a multivariate function and aimed to approximate it using a new layer
with flexible activation functions. To achieve this, they proposed an ALS algorithm to solve
the constrained coupled matrix-tensor factorization (CMTF) problem. This technique fuses
the first and zeroth-order information of the neural network, where the first-order information is captured in a Jacobian tensor, following a constrained CPD. By pointing out that
a drawback of the decoupling approach of [28], [29] is that it applies only to a single hidden
nonlinear layer, Konstantin et al. [30] proposed a two-layer decoupled representation that
includes two hidden layers. To retrieve the weights and the flexible activation functions,
they used an algorithm that is based on a coupled factorization of Jacobian and Hessian
tensors.
However, a notable limitation of current decoupling frameworks [28], [29], [30] (including the original multivariate function decoupling problem [28] and its follow-up works [29],
[30]) is that the input and output are strictly restricted to vector-valued. This constraint hinders its application to modern neural architectures such as Transformer-based DNNs [2]
and MLP-based DNNs [25], [26], which naturally operate on matrix-valued functions. To
the best of our knowledge, the problem of decoupling matrix-valued multivariate functions
has not yet been studied.
To fill in this gap, in this work [192], we formalize the problem of decoupling matrixvalued functions as a CMTF problem. We then propose to solve it via the ALS algorithm
by using both the first and zeroth-order information of the function. While the application of the decoupling matrix-valued function can be various, such as in signal processing,
system identification, and control engineering, we especially focus on its application in
DNNs compression. Specifically, we use it to compress matrix-valued architectures such
as Transformer-based DNNs [2] and MLP-based DNNs [25], [26]. Through extensive experiments on four architectures across seven datasets, the effectiveness of our approach is
demonstrated in terms of accuracy, parameter reduction, and MACs reduction, showcasing
its superiority over competing methods. For example, on ImageNet with DeiT, our method
achieves a 7% greater parameter reduction compared to SPViT-T [27], while also improving
accuracy by 0.28%.
– 104 –
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions Using Zeroth and First-Order Information
Our approach is the first, to the best of our knowledge, to generalize function decoupling to the matrix-valued setting and to demonstrate its practical utility for compressing large-scale matrix-valued DNNs. It offers several key distinctions from related work.
First, compared to previous decoupling frameworks [28], [29], [30], our method supports
matrix-valued inputs and outputs. Second, in contrast to standard low-rank approximation methods [31], [32], [33] that decompose layer weights, our approach approximates the
function mapping between input and output, without directly accessing weights. Third,
unlike pruning or quantization, our method works by approximating the function itself
through decomposition. This means it uses a different strategy and can be combined with
those methods to achieve even better compression. Briefly, the contributions of this work
are:
• Matrix–valued function decoupling. To the best of our knowledge, this work is
the first to explicitly formulate and study the decoupling of multivariate matrix–valued
nonlinear functions. We introduce Flex, a novel structured framework for matrix–valued
nonlinear mappings. The proposed model extends classical vector–valued decoupling
to the matrix setting by parameterizing the mapping through two linear transformation matrices and a matrix of univariate nonlinear functions. Consequently, the matrix–valued function is represented as a linear combination of univariate nonlinear
functions applied to linear projections of the input.
• Structured fifth-order tensor characterization. We derive the first-order information of the proposed model and prove that it admits a structured fifth-order tensor
representation with a coupled Kronecker delta–constrained Canonical Polyadic Decomposition (KdCPD). Importantly, the factor matrices of this CPD correspond exactly to the parameters of interest of the model. We further demonstrate that one of
these factors is shared with the zeroth-order information, which motivates formulating matrix–valued function decoupling as a coupled matrix–tensor factorization
problem.
• Constrained tensor-based learning algorithm. We propose a constrained alternating least squares algorithm with projection steps to perform the learning, which
in our framework amounts to decomposing coupled matrix–tensor structures. The
algorithm jointly exploits zeroth- and first-order information and enforces the model
constraints through projections, resulting in an efficient constrained tensor decomposition procedure.
• Application to neural network compression. We showcase the practical relevance of the proposed framework by applying matrix–valued function decoupling to
the compression of modern matrix–valued architectures. We evaluate our method
on four architectures across seven datasets and compare it with related works. We
further provide ablation studies and discussions on key aspects such as the rank and
the number of basis functions selection, and the impact of the chosen basis activation
function.
– 105 –
Chapter 7
7.2
The Flex Framework
In this section, we introduce the proposed Flex framework for decoupling matrix–valued
functions. The framework generalizes existing vector–valued decoupling approaches to
the intrinsically matrix–valued setting, while preserving structural interpretability and parameter efficiency. We first present the proposed matrix–valued decoupled representation
and highlight its advantages over naive vectorization strategies. We then exploit first-order
information through a structured Jacobian tensor, revealing a coupled CPD structure that
enables identifiability analysis. To resolve the inherent limitations of purely Jacobian-based
identification, we incorporate zeroth-order information and formulate the learning problem
as a constrained coupled matrix–tensor factorization (CMTF). Finally, we derive a tailored
constrained ALS algorithm for efficient parameter estimation.
7.2.1
Proposed Model for Decoupling Matrix–Valued Function
We now introduce a structured framework for decoupling matrix–valued nonlinear functions. While decoupling of vector–valued functions has been extensively studied and theoretically grounded, many modern learning problems—such as multi-channel signals [193],
multivariate time series [194], attention mechanisms [2], and matrix-based neural architectures [25], [158]—naturally involve matrix–valued mappings. Extending decoupling from
vectors to matrices is therefore not merely a notational generalization, but a necessary step
toward modeling structured data while preserving interpretability and parameter efficiency.
Our goal is to construct a representation that preserves the intrinsic matrix structure of
both inputs and outputs, while retaining the key advantages of classical decoupling: separation of linear projections and univariate nonlinearities, compactness, and interpretability.
To this end, we generalize the vector–valued decoupling framework [28], [29] to the matrix
setting.
In the vector case, a function f : RL → RN can be expressed as

f u) = W g(V⊤ u ,
(7.1)
where V ∈ RL×R , W ∈ RN ×R are linear transformation matrices and g : RR → RR is a
vector of univariate nonlinear functions. This representation is compact, interpretable, and
has been shown to be useful in vector–valued function decoupling [28], [29].
When the input and output are inherently matrix–valued, i.e., F : RL×M → RN ×M ,
a naive approach is to vectorize both the input and output matrices U ∈ RL×M and
Y ∈ RN ×M to Uvectorized ∈ RLM and Yvectorized ∈ RN M , respectively, and then apply the
vector–valued model. However, this approach presents two major limitations. First, vectorization ignores the inherent matrix–valued structure of the data, which may be semantically
meaningful. Second, it leads to a substantial increase in model complexity. Indeed, in order
to preserve expressivity across the M columns, a vectorized formulation typically requires
a latent representation whose dimension scales with M . As a result, the associated transformation matrices take the form Vvec ∈ R(LM )×(RM ) , and Wvec ∈ R(N M )×(RM ) , which yields
– 106 –
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions Using Zeroth and First-Order Information
a number of parameters that scales linearly with M in the input, output, and latent dimensions. In contrast, our proposed matrix–valued decoupling model employs transformation
matrices V ∈ RL×R and W ∈ RN ×R , whose sizes are independent of M . Therefore, when
considering only the transformation matrices, the ratio between the number of parameters
in the vectorized formulation and in the proposed matrix formulation is
M 2 R(L + N )
= M 2.
R(L + N )
That is, the naive vectorization increases the number of linear transformation parameters by a factor of M 2 compared to the proposed structured matrix–valued model. This
quadratic growth clearly illustrates the advantage of preserving the matrix structure. In
contrast, the proposed matrix–valued formulation yields a significantly more compact and
interpretable representation while explicitly preserving the matrix structure.
To overcome these limitations, we propose to preserve the matrix structure and introduce the following matrix–valued decoupled model. Let us consider a real multivariate
matrix–valued function, denoted by F : RL×M → RN ×M , and is defined as:

F(U) the transformations V and W and the mappings grm (xrm ) that constitute the parallel


objective of the function decoupling problem is to retrieve the transformation matrices V
and W and the univariate function G of the decoupled structure so that the two formats
(coupled and decoupled) are equivalent. Another constraint is that G must be represented
as a linear combination of predefined basis functions as expressed in (7.5).
The decoupled form offers several attractive properties. First, it tends to be efficient, in
terms of the number of parameters, compared to the coupled form, to represent the relationship between the input and output. The number of parameters can be explicitly controlled
through the hyperparameters R and D. Second, as the functions grm are univariate, they
are more tractable than the multivariate function of the coupled form. This can sometimes
lead to insight into the relationship between the input and the output. Third, the kind of
basis functions of grm is not necessarily fixed. They can be chosen as per context, for example, one can use the polynomial basis in the case of this dataset, while another may use
the piece-wise linear basis in the case of another dataset. It is worth noting that the decoupled structure can be seen as a neural network, with V, W, and C acting as weights, while
grm is the activation function, with ϕd is often chosen as a nonlinear function. Under this
view, efficiency corresponds to the reduced number of parameters, interpretability arises
from the univariate structure of grm , and flexibility stems from the customizable choice of
basis functions. Moreover, in the context of neural networks, the weights of the decoupled
form can be learned using tensor decompositions, as will be shown later. This is particularly useful in scenarios where gradient-based training (e.g., backpropagation) is restricted
or expensive. Nonetheless, the decoupled form remains compatible with backpropagation.
Overall, this makes function decoupling a promising and practical tool for neural network
learning and compression.
– 108 –
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions Using Zeroth and First-Order Information
7.2.2
First-Order Information
Following the common strategy for vector–valued function decoupling introduced in [28],
[29], we exploit first-order information of the matrix–valued function F through its Jacobian. However, due to the intrinsically matrix–valued nature of the proposed model,
the resulting Jacobian exhibits a richer tensorial structure than in the vector case. Importantly, this structure is not an arbitrary construction but is induced by the decoupled
matrix–valued formulation itself.
Consider the matrix–valued function F : RL×M → RN ×M . Differentiating each output
entry fnm with respect to each input entry ulp naturally yields a fourth-order Jacobian
tensor J ∈ RN ×M ×L×M . This tensorial order directly reflects the matrix–valued nature of
both the input and the output. We first characterize the Jacobian at a single point.

interpreted as a structured collection of coupled CPDs rather than as a single global CPD.
This phenomenon does not arise in the classical vector–valued decoupling framework and
is a direct consequence of the matrix–valued formulation.

Equations (7.12) and (7.13) allow one to determine the coefficients of functions grm from
the retrieved CPD factors W, H(b) , and V of J (b) .
It is worth noting that the decomposition in (7.12) is not merely the application of a
standard CPD to an arbitrary tensor. Rather, it results from a specific tensorial structure
induced by the matrix–valued nature of the mapping F : RL×M → RN ×M and by the decoupled functional form of the proposed model. Each tensor J (b) admits a CPD with shared
factor matrices W and V across all operating points, while the matrix factor H(b) varies
with b and encodes the evaluations of the derivatives of the univariate nonlinear functions.
As a result, the full Jacobian information across all operating points can be interpreted as
a structured collection of coupled CPDs, rather than as independent decompositions. This
coupling is a key property that enables the consistent recovery of the linear projection
matrices and justifies the proposed decoupling strategy.
To analyze identifiability, the classical uniqueness results for the CPD can be projected
onto each tensor J (b) . Under mild conditions, essential uniqueness holds for the decomposition in (7.12). In particular, following the Kruskal theorem [195], the CPD of J (b) uniquely
decomposes J (b) into the factors W, H(b) , and V (up to a permutation and scaling of the
columns), provided that

krank(W) + krank H(b) + krank(V) ≥ 2R + 2,
(7.14)
where krank(·) denotes the Kruskal rank [195] of a matrix. If the factor matrices W, H(b) ,
and V have full column rank, condition (7.14) boils down to
min(N, R) + min(M, R) + min(L, R) ≥ 2R + 2.
(7.15)
In this case, the factors W and V are identifiable up to the usual scaling and permutation
ambiguities. The identifiability of W and V implies that the internal univariate nonlinear
functions grm (·) can be reconstructed (up to additive constants) provided sufficiently rich
sampling of the projected inputs. Indeed, once the linear
are recovered, the en projections

⊤
(b)

grm can be reconstructed up to an additive constant. Therefore, the essential uniqueness of
the CPD factors provides a theoretical justification for the proposed decoupling approach
and explains why the model parameters can be consistently recovered from first-order information.
– 111 –
Chapter 7
However, it is important to note that identifiability from J (b) is inherently limited to
⊤

first-order information. While the derivatives grm
(·) can be recovered from the factor H(b) ,
the additive constant in each univariate nonlinear function grm (·), i.e., crm,0 in Eq (7.5), is
eliminated by differentiation and therefore cannot be identified from the Jacobian alone.
Consequently, even under the essential uniqueness of the CPD and perfect recovery of the
factors W, H(b) , and V, the functions grm (·) can only be reconstructed up to an unknown
constant offset. This results in an intrinsic bias in the recovered input–output mapping
when relying exclusively on first-order information. Such an ambiguity is fundamental to
any Jacobian-based identification approach and cannot be resolved without incorporating
information beyond derivatives [29]. To overcome this limitation, we propose in Subsection 7.2.3 to combine first-order and zeroth-order information, thereby addressing the bias
issue and improving reconstruction accuracy.
7.2.3
Zeroth-Order Information and Formulation of the Problem
In contrast to classical system identification settings, where only derivative information
may be available, in our context, the proposed decoupling approach is applied to pretrained
neural networks, and the original subnetwork can be directly evaluated at arbitrary sampling points. Consequently, zeroth-order information is naturally accessible and can be
exploited. Therefore, in addition to the Jacobian tensors J (b) , we can also collect the function evaluations at B different sampling points U(b) ∈ RL×M . This additional information
enables us to resolve the constant-term ambiguity and to formulate the learning problem
as a coupled matrix–tensor factorization problem that jointly exploits first- and zerothorder information. We will show that matrix Y(b) shares the common factor W with the
first-order tensor J (b) , which naturally leads to a coupled decomposition. The proposed
approach fuses the first and zeroth order information, which allows us to improve the performance. We will formulate the learning problem as a constrained CMTF problem where
we factorize both the first and zeroth order information, while constraining the nonlinear
activation functions to follow the form proposed in Eq. (7.5).
Let Y(b) ∈ RN ×M denote thematrix collecting the function evaluations at the b sampling point, i.e., Y(b) = F U(b) corresponds to the output of the original subnetwork
evaluated at U(b) . From the decoupled representation in (7.3), each slice Y(b) admits the
factorization

where Z(b) ∈ RR×M is the matrix collecting the output of the nonlinear activation functions

Based on this observation, we therefore propose to use the zeroth-order information Y(b)

the transformation matrices V, W, and the nonlinear functions grm (·) (more particularly,
the coefficient C) of the proposed decoupled model.
We propose to formulate the learning problem as a constrained CMTF problem that
jointly decomposes J (b) and Y(b) while enforcing the flexible activation functions to follow
the parametric form in (7.5). The resulting optimization problem is
