ensuring that both first- and zeroth-order information are consistently parameterized by
the same coefficient vector crm . Solving the criterion (7.17) allows us to retrieve the transformation matrices V, W, and the coefficient C of the proposed decoupled model from
both tensor J (b) and matrix Y(b) , while forcing the nonlinear activation function grm (·)
to follow the constraint in (7.5). It is worth noting that the use of matrix Y(b) allows us
– 113 –
Chapter 7
to estimate the constant terms crm,0 in grm , contained in matrix Z(b) , which is not the case
with relying only on tensor J (b) .
The criterion in (7.17) is non-convex due to the multilinear structure of the factors and
the coupling constraints between the different components. Such problems are standard
in tensor decomposition and coupled matrix–tensor factorization, and can be addressed
using nonlinear least-squares techniques that update all variables jointly via gradient-based
methods [67], [196], [197]. Alternatively, block-coordinate descent strategies update one
group of variables at a time while keeping the others fixed, which is a well-established
approach for structured non-convex optimization problems [198], [199].
In the tensor decomposition literature, alternating least squares (ALS) is one of the most
widely adopted block-coordinate descent schemes for CPD and CMTF models [67], [200],
[201], [202]. When all variables except one block are fixed, the subproblems with respect
to W, V, and the coefficient tensors reduce to (constrained) linear least-squares problems.
This structure enables efficient updates with closed-form or numerically stable solutions,
while naturally handling the coupling between J (b) and Y(b) . Moreover, due to the multilinear structure of the CPD model, each subproblem admits a closed-form least-squares
solution, which guarantees a monotonic decrease of the objective at each iteration under
exact least-squares updates [67]. For these reasons, and given its widespread use and robustness in CPD and CMTF frameworks, ALS constitutes a suitable and computationally
efficient strategy for solving (7.17).
7.2.4
Proposed Constrained Alternating Least Squares Algorithm
To solve the optimization problem (7.17), we propose a constrained ALS algorithm summarized in Algorithm 6. The method follows a block-coordinate descent strategy, where the
objective function in (7.17) is minimized with respect to one group of variables at a time,
while keeping the others fixed. Ignoring for the moment the structural constraints on H(b)
and Z(b) , the multilinear structure of the CPD model implies that the subproblems with
respect to W, H(b) , V (see Eq. (7.12)), and Z(b) (see Eq. (7.16)) reduce to linear least-squares
problems. These admit closed-form solutions expressed using pseudoinverses. This corresponds to a classical ALS scheme for coupled matrix–tensor factorization. To enforce the
structure induced by the activation function parametrization (7.5), we introduce an additional projection step. After the unconstrained ALS updates, the vectors hmr and zrm are
jointly re-estimated through a coefficient vector crm obtained by solving a structured leastsquares problem. This step can be interpreted as a projection onto the subspace spanned
by the activation basis functions {ϕd }D
d=1 . Consequently, the algorithm alternates between
standard ALS updates and structured projections, resulting in a constrained ALS-type procedure tailored to (7.17).
Algorithm 6 consists of two main phases at each iteration. In Phase I (Lines 4–7), the
factors are updated using standard ALS-type least-squares subproblems derived from (7.17).
These updates temporarily ignore the structural constraints imposed on H(b) and Z(b) and
correspond to an unconstrained coupled matrix–tensor factorization step. In Phase II (Lines 13–
15), the activation-function structure is enforced. For each pair (r, m), the coefficient vec– 114 –

In summary, the proposed Flex framework extends decoupled representations to matrix–valued functions by exploiting the structured CPD of the Jacobian tensor and coupling
it with zeroth-order information. The resulting constrained CMTF formulation jointly estimates the linear transformations and nonlinear activation functions while resolving the
constant ambiguity inherent to derivative-based identification. By incorporating the parametric activation structure through a projection step within an ALS scheme, the method
ensures consistent parametrization of both first- and zeroth-order terms. This yields an efficient and structurally coherent approach for learning decoupled matrix–valued mappings
from sampled data.
7.3
Experiments
7.3.1
Experimental Setup
Architectures and Datasets: Flex is evaluated on 4 architectures with matrix-valued inputs and outputs: ViT [49], DeiT [158], Mixer [25], and ResMLP [26]. ViT and DeiT are wellknown models in the vision transformer family, while Mixer and ResMLP are included to
– 116 –
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions Using Zeroth and First-Order Information
demonstrate Flex’s applicability to non-transformer architectures. To evaluate generality,
we test on seven datasets of varying scale and complexity: MNIST [52], CIFAR [53], ImageNet [36], QMNIST [69], SVHN [72], FashionMNIST [70], and USPS [71]. Each experiment
is conducted at several compression levels.
Configuration: For MNIST [52], QMNIST [69], SVHN [72], FashionMNIST [70], and USPS
[71], we fine-tune the compressed models using a batch size of 16, a learning rate of 1e−4,
and 20 fine-tuning epochs. For CIFAR [53], we use a batch size of 128, a learning rate of
5e−4, and 200 fine-tuning epochs. For ImageNet, we adopt the training configuration from
DeiT [158]: 300 epochs, a cosine learning rate scheduler with an initial learning rate of 0.03,
and a batch size of 128. We conducted fine-tuning using NVIDIA Tesla V100 SXM2 16GB
GPUs and Intel Cascade Lake 6248 processors (20 cores at 2.5 GHz).
Baselines: We compare Flex against 16 related methods, including the 4 original architectures—ViT [49], DeiT [158], Mixer [25], and ResMLP [26]—as well as 12 recent compression
techniques: Hajimolahoseini et al. [31], LASER [32], LRT [33], Lightformer [203], SPViT-T
[27], SSP-T [204], [205], S2 ViTE-T [206], SCOP [166], PoWER [207], HVT [208], and WDPruning [209]. We follow the evaluation protocols described in Subsection 3.3.1.
7.3.2
Results and Comparisons
Results on ViT
MNIST. Table 7.1 presents the compression results of ViT [49] on the MNIST dataset [52].
Notably, at a moderate compression level (51% MACs, 47% parameters), Flex even improves
the top-1 accuracy over the uncompressed model, reaching 98.31% compared to the original
98.24%. Our method consistently achieves higher top-1 accuracy than prior approaches,
while also yielding superior compression in both MACs and parameters. Compared to
the low-rank decomposition strategies of Hajimolahoseini et al. [31], Flex provides better
trade-offs across all compression levels. For instance, at a MACs compression ratio of 60%,
Flex improves accuracy from 97.86% to 98.22% while using fewer parameters. Compared to
LASER [32], Flex achieves a slightly higher accuracy (98.31% vs. 98.26%) with fewer MACs
(12.79M vs. 14.44M) and parameters (844K vs. 864K). Notably, Flex can compress more than
90% of both the number of parameters and MACs of the original model while introducing
just a 0.31% drop in accuracy (97.93% vs. 98.24%). This result outperforms LRT [33] by over
0.5% while using fewer parameters (139K vs. 143K). These results confirm Flex’s ability to
maintain or even improve performance under aggressive model compression.

Results on Mixer
Mixer [25] is an architecture based purely on multi-layer perceptrons, demonstrating that
MLP-only models can serve as effective alternatives to convolutional and transformer-based
networks. It comprises per-patch linear embeddings, stacked Mixer layers, and a classification head. Each Mixer layer includes a token-mixing and a channel-mixing MLP, both
consisting of two fully connected layers with GELU activations. In our experiments, we apply the Flex method to compress the Mixer layers, leaving the embedding and classification
layers untouched.

Mixer, achieving substantial efficiency gains with minimal performance loss.

Results on ResMLP
ResMLP [26] is an MLP-based architecture for image classification that eliminates selfattention, positional embeddings, and the additional “class” token used in ViT. It comprises
a linear projection for input embedding, a stack of ResMLP layers, and a classification head.
Each ResMLP layer includes a cross-patch linear sublayer and a cross-channel two-layer
MLP, both equipped with residual connections and affine element-wise transformations. In
our experiments, we apply the Flex method to compress only the matrix-valued ResMLP
layers, leaving the embedding and classification components unchanged.

7.4
Discussions
7.4.1
The Impact of the Rank and the Number of Basis Functions
Selection
As the rank R and the number of basis functions D directly influence the compression rate,
approximation errors, and accuracies of the compressed model, we conduct additional ex– 121 –
Chapter 7
periments on a ViT model [49] pre-trained on the MNIST dataset [52] to investigate the effect of these hyperparameter selections. We use the Normalized Mean Square Error (NMSE)
between the estimated Jˆ or F̂ and the original ones to evaluate the quality of the decomposition, defined as
Jˆ − J
2
F
NMSE(J ) =
∥J ∥2F
,
and
2
F̂ − F
NMSE(F ) =
F
∥F ∥2F
.
Compression rate is reflected through reductions in the number of parameters. Additionally, we report the accuracy drop of the decomposed model without and with fine-tuning.
As the range of R is wide, we thus choose R as R = 16i, for 1 ≤ i ≤ 16, and 1 ≤ D ≤ 4 to
limit the number of testing cases. Figure 7.2 visualizes the number of parameters reduction,
approximation errors, and accuracies concerning the combinations of R and D. The results
support the following claims.
The (R, D) combination controls a wide spectrum of compression rates. Our experiments demonstrate that varying (R, K) leads to a broad range of MACs and parameter
reductions, spanning from about -8% to 95%. The flexibility in selecting R and D allows
practitioners to select configurations that meet specific complexity reduction goals.
Flex performs well without fine-tuning in moderate compression settings. We observe
that Flex maintains good accuracy (less than 20% accuracy drop) even without fine-tuning,
particularly in configurations where R ≥ 32, where the NMSE(F ) < 0.4. In these cases,
the accuracy drop remains under 20%. Especially, when R ≥ 64, we have NMSE(F ) < 0.1,
and the accuracy drop remains under 2.5%. For instance, with R = 128 and D = 3, Flex
reduces about 50% of the number of parameters while yielding an accuracy drop of only
about 0.8%. This suggests that our method can be used in contexts where fine-tuning is not
feasible, such as when computational resources are limited, when there is a lack of training
data, or when rapid deployment is necessary.
Flex effectively works when followed by a fine-tuning step. Across all configurations,
when we conduct a fine-tuning on the decomposed model, the accuracy drop is only less
than around 0.6%. For example, with R = 32 and D = 2, Flex reduces the number of
parameters by about 90% with no accuracy drop. In some cases, fine-tuned models even
outperform the original, suggesting that the original model is over-parameterized.
A trend emerges between NMSEs and the values of R and D: NMSEs tend to decrease
as either R or D increases. In other words, at fixed R, increasing D lowers NMSEs, and
similarly, increasing R at fixed D has the same effect. This suggests a trade-off: higher
model complexity typically results in lower approximation error. Note that NMSE(F )
and accuracy without fine-tuning are negatively correlated. Additionally, R and D are
positively correlated with the number of parameters of the compact model. Therefore, we
can infer that in some cases, the number of parameters and accuracy without fine-tuning
are positively correlated.
– 122 –
Decoupling Matrix-Valued Functions Using Zeroth and First-Order Information
Given a target complexity reduction, multiple combinations of R and D yield comparable compressions but with different accuracies. For example, with a target parameter
reduction of 50%, there are at least three combinations of (R, D) that satisfy the objective:
(128, 3), (144, 2), and (160, 1) with the number of parameters reductions are 50%, 49%, and
48%, respectively. Their accuracy drops after fine-tuning are 0.31%, 0.31%, and 0.17%, respectively. These combinations also result in different architectures with varying numbers
of neurons and activation functions. We argue that selecting the optimal combination of
R and D based on a compression objective can be viewed as an optimal hyperparameter
configuration or architecture search problem. NMSEs can be suitable indicators for this
problem. We leave this problem for future works as we mainly focus on the decomposition
scheme in this work.

7.4.3
Effectiveness of the Decomposition Step in Flex
In this subsection, we evaluate the advantages of the decomposition step in Flex compared
to the conventional training-from-scratch approach. We conduct experiments using a ViT
model [49] pre-trained on the CIFAR-10 dataset [52], which achieves an original accuracy
of 75.06%. For the Flex method, we approximate the transformer block via CMTF and then
fine-tune the model. In contrast, the training-from-scratch baseline uses Kaiming initialization [210], a widely adopted weight initialization technique, and trains the model directly.
To ensure a fair comparison, we keep all hyperparameters identical across both methods:
the rank (R = 256), the number of basis functions (D = 2), the type of basis activation function (polynomial), and the total number of training epochs (200). At epoch 0 (before any fine-tuning, or after the decomposition step), the Flex model achieves a validation
accuracy of 57.69%, while the Kaiming-initialized model reaches only 9.39%. This demonstrates that Flex preserves useful information from the pre-trained model even without any
additional training, highlighting its potential for settings where fine-tuning resources are
limited. After fine-tuning, as illustrated in Figure 7.4, Flex demonstrates both faster convergence and superior final accuracy. It reaches a validation accuracy of 73.41% after just 20
epochs and peaks at 74.29% after 100 epochs. In contrast, the training-from-scratch model
only approaches 73.21% by the end of 200 epochs. These results highlight the effectiveness
of Flex in accelerating learning through the integration of prior knowledge. In terms of
computational cost, the decomposition step in Flex requires 909 seconds. The subsequent
fine-tuning phase for both methods takes approximately 14801 seconds in total, averaging 74 seconds per epoch. While Flex incurs a small additional cost due to decomposition,
this overhead is compensated by faster convergence and improved final performance. Notably, if training is stopped at Flex’s peak accuracy (epoch 100), the total runtime is only
8309 seconds—including decomposition—which is significantly less than the full training
time of the baseline. Finally, our findings are consistent with prior works on low-rank decomposition [10], which suggest that fine-tuning a well-approximated model can lead to
improved accuracy and faster convergence compared to training from scratch.
7.5
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have introduced a novel framework, Flex, for decoupling matrix-valued
multivariate functions using constrained coupled matrix-tensor factorization. Unlike existing decoupling approaches that are limited to vector-valued functions, our method generalizes the concept to matrix-valued mappings and proposes an ALS-based solution that leverages both zeroth-order and first-order information. We have demonstrated the utility of this
formulation in the context of deep neural network compression, targeting matrix-valued
architectures such as Vision Transformers and MLP-based networks. Comprehensive experiments across four architectures and seven datasets show that Flex achieves substantial
reductions in model size and computational cost, often improving or preserving accuracy
compared to uncompressed baselines and outperforming several state-of-the-art compression methods. We also provided in-depth analyses of the hyperparameters—rank and number of basis functions—and their influence on compression rate and accuracy. Overall, Flex

opens a new direction in the study of function approximation and model compression by
extending decoupling techniques to the matrix-valued setting. Future work may explore
automatic selection of hyperparameters such as rank and number of basis functions, extensions to multi-layer decoupled representation, and applications beyond neural network
compression, including nonlinear system identification.
– 126 –
Chapter 8
Conclusions and Perspectives
This final chapter synthesizes the main contributions of the thesis and outlines potential
avenues for future research. We first provide a concise summary of the methods and findings presented in the previous chapters, highlighting their significance in advancing the
field of deep neural network compression. We then discuss perspectives for extending this
work, considering both methodological innovations and emerging application domains. By
reflecting on the limitations and open challenges identified throughout the thesis, we aim
to position our contributions within the evolving landscape of efficient deep learning and
to suggest promising directions for future exploration.
8.1
Conclusions
This thesis has addressed the pressing challenge of deep neural network compression in the
context of increasing model sizes, computational demands, and deployment constraints. We
proposed a series of novel approaches to structured compression, grounded in the mathematical principles of low-rank approximations.
Our contributions span several complementary directions. First, we have developed
CORING, a tensor factorization-based pruning method that leverages the HOSVD structure
of convolutional filters. By preserving the multi-linear structure inherent to these filters,
CORING achieves effective compression with minimal performance degradation. Second,
we have proposed SLIMING, a method for pruning filters based on spectral information.
This approach utilizes singular value analysis to inform both the pruning configuration
and filter selection, enabling a more principled and automated compression process. Third,
we have introduced NORTON, a unified framework that combines tensor decomposition
and structured pruning through the notion of filter decomposition. This method facilitates
a fine-grained trade-off between compression rate and model accuracy across various architectures and datasets. Fourth, we have presented CoDeC, a coupled decomposition strategy
that jointly factorizes groups of similar filters using coupled CPD. A custom clustering algorithm is employed to group filters prior to decomposition, improving representation compactness and reducing redundancy. Finally, we have proposed Flex, a general framework
127
Chapter 8
for approximating matrix-valued functions, which enables the compression of architectures
beyond CNNs, such as Transformers and MLP-Mixers. This method formulates the compression problem as a coupled matrix-tensor factorization and solves it via an alternating
least squares algorithm that incorporates both zeroth- and first-order information.
Our evaluation protocols, based on common vision datasets and architectures, were
designed to be reproducible and consistent across all contributions. These protocols have
been systematically applied throughout the thesis, facilitating a fair comparison between
methods and ensuring the generality of the proposed approaches.
Taken together, the findings in this thesis contribute to the broader effort to make deep
learning models more efficient, scalable, and accessible for real-world applications, while
maintaining accuracy and generalization with minimal compromise.
8.2
Perspectives
Several promising directions emerge from this work for future research:
• Automated compression hyperparameter optimization: A notable limitation
of our current methods lies in the manual, layerwise selection of compression hyperparameters, such as pruning ratios (CORING, NORTON), decomposition ranks
(NORTON, CoDeC, Flex), cluster counts (CoDeC), and the number of basis functions
(Flex). This layer-local tuning does not guarantee global optimality, as layers vary
in importance and interact to influence overall model performance. Techniques like
NAS or reinforcement learning could automate the discovery of optimal hyperparameter configurations tailored to specific architectures and datasets.
• Extension to diverse architectures: The generality of the matrix-valued function
approximation approach in Flex suggests promising applications beyond CNNs, notably for architectures such as Transformers and MLP-Mixers. Future research can
focus on compressing models in domains like natural language processing, time series
analysis, and multi-modal learning, where architectural characteristics differ substantially.
• Hybrid compression frameworks: Building on the pruning and tensor decomposition methods developed in this thesis, future work could explore integrating these
approaches with complementary compression techniques such as knowledge distillation, quantization, and NAS. Combining multiple methods in a unified framework
has the potential to leverage their respective strengths, achieving higher compression
rates and better accuracy retention. Such hybrid strategies would also benefit from
end-to-end optimization and hardware-awareness, enabling practical deployment on
diverse platforms.
• Hardware-aware compression: Future work could also consider hardware constraints more explicitly, incorporating platform-specific factors such as memory hierarchy, parallelism, and power consumption into the design of compression algorithms.
– 128 –
• Training-aware, continual, and federated compression: Incorporating compression into the training process opens new avenues for efficient and adaptive model
deployment. In continual learning scenarios, where models must adapt incrementally to non-stationary data streams, training-aware compression can help manage
capacity while preserving knowledge over time. In federated learning, where training occurs across distributed, resource-constrained devices under privacy constraints,
compression is essential to reduce communication overhead and enable efficient ondevice computation. Future research could develop lightweight, adaptive compression strategies that operate seamlessly during training, while accounting for the unique
challenges of both continual and federated settings.
In summary, while this thesis has made significant progress in developing mathematically grounded, efficient, and effective compression techniques, the landscape of model
compression continues to evolve. The increasing diversity of models, tasks, and deployment
environments makes it essential to continue developing flexible, adaptive, and theoretically
justified solutions for network compression.

Van Tien PHAM
Laboratoire d’Informatique et des Systèmes, Université de Toulon
Compression des Réseaux de Neurones Profonds par Élagage et Approximations de Rang Faible
Cette thèse présente cinq méthodes de compression de réseaux de neurones, en se concentrant sur le pruning et les approximations de rang réduit. Premièrement, CORING (effiCient tensOr decomposition-based filteR prunING) est une méthode de pruning efficace
qui utilise la décomposition en valeurs singulières d’ordre supérieur pour préserver la structure tensorielle des filtres convolutionnels, permettant des approximations de rang faible
efficaces. Deuxièmement, SLIMING (Singular vaLues-drIven autoMated filter prunING) est
un cadre piloté par les valeurs singulières qui formalise le pruning comme un problème
d’optimisation en deux étapes, où la configuration et la sélection des filtres sont guidées
par des propriétés spectrales. Troisièmement, NORTON (enhanced Network cOmpRession
through TensOr decompositions and pruNing) est une approche hybride qui combine les
avantages de la décomposition tensorielle et du pruning structuré. Quatrièmement, CoDeC
(Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation) utilise la décomposition canonique polyadique couplée pour factoriser conjointement des groupes de filtres
similaires, avec une métrique de similarité personnalisée utilisée pour le pré-clustering.
Enfin, Flex étend la compression aux fonctions multivariées à valeurs matricielles via un
cadre de découplage formulé comme un problème de factorisation matrice–tenseur couplée,
résolu par moindres carrés alternés utilisant des informations d’ordre zéro et un. Ensemble, ces contributions offrent des stratégies complémentaires et rigoureuses pour réduire la
complexité des modèles tout en maintenant des performances élevées sur diverses architectures de réseaux de neurones.
Mots clés: approximations de rang faible, élagage, compression des réseaux de neurones.
*******************************
Deep Neural Network Compression Using Pruning and Low-Rank Approximations
This thesis introduces five methods for neural network compression, focusing on pruning
and low-rank approximations. First, CORING (effiCient tensOr decomposition-based filteR
prunING) is an efficient pruning method that uses higher-order singular value decomposition to preserve the tensorial structure of convolutional filters, enabling effective low-rank
approximations. Second, SLIMING (Singular vaLues-drIven autoMated filter prunING) is
a singular value-driven framework that formulates pruning as a two-stage optimization
problem, where both configuration and filter selection are guided by spectral properties.
Third, NORTON (enhanced Network cOmpRession through TensOr decompositions and
pruNing) is a hybrid approach that leverages the advantages of both tensor decomposition
and structured pruning. Fourth, CoDeC (Coupled tensor Decomposition for Compact network representation) employs coupled canonical polyadic decomposition to jointly factorize groups of similar filters, with a custom similarity metric used for pre-clustering. Finally,
Flex extends compression to matrix-valued multivariate functions through a decoupling
framework formulated as a coupled matrix–tensor factorization problem, solved via alternating least squares with zeroth- and first-order information. Together, these contributions
offer complementary and principled strategies to reduce model complexity while maintaining high performance across diverse neural architectures.
Keywords: low-rank approximations, pruning, neural network compression.
